Breaking the symmetry in excess intersection and an application to counting solutions of systems of polynomials
Pinaki Mondal

Abstract
. Given n n regular functions f 1 , … , f n f_{1},\ldots,f_{n} on an n n -dimensional variety X X , and a subset Z Z of X X , we consider deformations of the set of zeroes of the f i f_{i} of the form f 1 − t r 1 ​ g 1 = ⋯ = f n − t r n ​ g n = 0 f_{1}-t^{r_{1}}g_{1}=\cdots=f_{n}-t^{r_{n}}g_{n}=0 where the g i g_{i} are generic, and study the number N Z N_{Z} of points which “move to” generic points of Z Z as t t moves to 0 0 . We compute N Z N_{Z} in the case that each r i / r i + 1 ≫ 1 r_{i}/r_{i+1}\gg 1 . This associates to every subvariety of X X an asymmetric variant of the dynamic intersection number defined by Lazarsfeld [Compos. Math. 43, 1981] à la Severi [Ann. Mat. Pura Appl. 26 (4), 1947]. We explicitly describe the “distinguished components” of this “ordered intersection” (i.e. all Z Z such that N Z > 0 N_{Z}>0 ), and give an explicit formula for N Z N_{Z} in terms of sums of products of various “degrees” of Z Z (the “global” factors) and lengths of certain ideals in the local ring of Z Z (the “local” factors). We also consider certain scenarios in which N Z N_{Z} can be computed in terms of associated Newton diagrams, and describe how these point towards inductive solutions to the affine Bézout problem of counting isolated solutions of systems of polynomials. To illustrate our methods we compute the number of common tangent lines to 4 4 general spheres in 𝕜 3 \mathbbm{k}^{3} (which is known to be 12 due to Macdonald, Pach, and Theobald [Discrete Comput. Geom. 26 (1), 2001]) via certain ordered intersection multiplicities on the corresponding Grassmannian.



1.
Introduction

This work is motivated by the
affine Bézout problem
of counting the precise number of isolated solutions of a system
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
of
n
polynomials in
n
variables over an algebraically closed field
\mathbbm{k}
. A general approach to this problem is to consider appropriate vector spaces of polynomials containing
f_{i}
, which corresponds to embedding the
f_{i}
in families of base-point free linear systems
\mathcal{L}_{i}
(on a “natural” ambient space). In the generic case the given system is “non-degenerate”, and the number of its solutions equals the number of common solutions of generic divisors
G_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{i}
, which is calculated by a version of Bézout’s theorem or Bernstein-Kushnirenko theorem. However, if the system is “degenerate”, then this calculated bound is not sharp, and the intersection of the (effective) divisors
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
corresponding to the given polynomials contains “problematic” points (which could be “points at infinity” or non-isolated components). This leads one to the following general problem:

Problem 1.1
.

Given a subset
Z
of
\bigcap_{i}\supp(F_{i})
, count (with appropriate multiplicity) how many of the points in
\bigcap_{i}\supp(G_{i})
“move to”
Z
as the
G_{i}
“move to”
F_{i}
(where
G_{i}
are generic elements of
\mathcal{L}_{i}
).

In the first part of this article we describe an explicit solution to
1.1
in terms of
ordered intersection multiplicities
. The point is that these ordered intersection multiplicities and corresponding “distinguished components” (i.e. the varieties along which the ordered intersection multiplicity is nonzero) admit fairly explicit and elementary descriptions. In the second part we describe certain scenarios in which the ordered intersection multiplicities can be estimated in terms of associated “Newton diagrams”. To illustrate our methods from the second part, we compute the number of common tangent lines to
4
general spheres in
\mathbbm{k}^{3}
(which is known to be 12 due to Macdonald, Pach, and Theobald
[
MPT01
]
) by computing ordered intersection multiplicities of some relevant excess intersections on the Grassmannian of lines in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
.

1.2
.
The big picture in the context of the affine Bézout problem

A problem associated to, and deeply intertwined with, the affine Bézout problem is that of computing the multiplicity of a (proper) isolated intersection at a nonsingular point. In the “non-degenerate” case this multiplicity can be estimated in terms of associated Newton diagrams, and in the “degenerate” case this multiplicity is equal to the non-degenerate multiplicity plus the multiplicity of certain excess intersections on a blow up at the point (see e.g.
[
Mon21
, Chapter IX]
).

1.2.1
.

In turn, the “ordered intersection multiplicities” can be expressed in terms of the (usual) intersection multiplicities of divisors at isolated points of intersections on a birational modification of the given variety (this modification can be described in terms of the given
F_{i}
and “generic”
G_{i}
from
1.1
- see
Section

10.1
). In a sense this provides an inductive “solution” to the affine Bézout problem: start with the non-degenerate bound; if there are excess intersections, then express the corresponding ordered multiplicities in terms of the usual intersection multiplicities of divisors at isolated points of intersection on an appropriate modification; then compute the “non-degenerate portion” of the latter multiplicities in terms of Newton diagrams and express the “degenerate portion” in terms of excess intersections, and then repeat. We are however not sure about the practicality or complexity of this “solution.”

1.2.2
.

There are similar inductive routes to the affine Bézout problem when
\bigcup_{i}\supp(F_{i})
is the support of a strict normal crossing divisor, i.e. when resolution of singularities is available (e.g. in characteristic zero), or when certain curves are locally set-theoretic complete intersections (which is true in positive characteristics by a result of Cowsik and Nori
[
CN78
]
) - see
Sections

10.2
and
10.3
.

1.2.3
.

In general it is unknown if every curve is a local set-theoretic complete intersection. Short of the birational “modifications” mentioned in
Sections

1.2.1
and
1.2.2
, we do not know of any general method to obtain combinatorial or convex geometric estimates of the ordered intersection multiplicity if the associated curves are not set-theoretic complete intersection near the corresponding “centers”. Sometimes it is possible to give such an estimate when a “punctured” germ of the curve is a complete intersection in the complement of a union of “coordinate subspaces” (see
Section

8.3
); in order to extend this approach to a general method one would need to get a handle on the
tropicalization
of a curve
C
near a point
P
, which seems to be a hard problem if
C
is not a (set-theoretic) complete intersection near
P
.

1.3
.

In
Section

2
we provide a detailed introduction to the main results of this article. The subsequent sections are divided into two largely independent parts: in the first part (comprising of
Sections

3
to
6
) we show that the “ordered intersection multiplicity” is well defined and find an explicit formula for it; the second part (spanning
Sections

7
to
10
) provides various expressions of the ordered intersection multiplicity in terms of usual intersection multiplicities of divisors, and resulting estimates in terms of
Newton diagrams
. Note that for applications to the affine Bézout problem one does not need the dynamic interpretation of ordered intersection multiplicity - it suffices to read only the proof of
5.7
in
Section

5
, and then
Sections

7
to
10
in the second part.

2.
An overview of ordered intersection multiplicity and the main results

2.1
.
Ordered intersection multiplicity

Consider the set up of
1.1
. More precisely, let
X
be a variety of dimension
n
, and
F_{i}
be an effective Cartier divisor on
X
which is an element of a base-point free linear system
\mathcal{L}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
.
For each choice of
G_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, we denote by
C^{*}=C^{*}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{n};r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})
the subscheme of
X\times(\mathbbm{k}\setminus\{0\})
which is defined locally as
V(f_{1}-t^{r_{1}}g_{1},\ldots,f_{n}-t^{r_{n}}g_{n})
where
f_{i},g_{i}
are local representatives of respectively
F_{i},G_{i}
. If
G_{1},\ldots,G_{n}
are generic, then for almost all
a\in\mathbbm{k}
, the number of points on
C^{*}
at
t=a
, counted with appropriate multiplicities, is precisely the intersection number
(\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n})
. Consequently,
C^{*}
can be viewed as a moving collection of
(\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n})
points, and it seems reasonable, as Severi
[
Sev47
]
suggested, to count how many of these points approach “generic” points of a given subvariety
Z\subseteq X
, and take it as a measure of the intersection multiplicity of
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
along
Z
; we informally denote this number as
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\vec{r}}
(where
\vec{r}:=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})
). In the case that
X
is nonsingular, Lazarsfeld
[
Laz81
]
rigorously developed this notion for
\vec{r}=(1,\ldots,1)
following (and correcting) the idea of Severi
[
Sev47
]
1
1

1

Severi
[
Sev47
]
suggested taking the
minimum
possible number of points approaching generic points of
Z
. Lazarsfeld
[
Laz81
]
showed that one should instead take the number of points in a
generic
such deformation.
. The point of departure for this article is the observation that
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\vec{r}}
admits an explicit description in the extreme limit that
r_{1}\gg r_{2}\gg\cdots\gg r_{n}>0
; motivated by this somewhat of a miracle we consider the
ordered intersection multiplicity

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle:=\lim_{\min\{\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}},\ldots,\frac{r_{n-1}}{r_{n}}\}\to\infty}(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\vec{r}}

We show that
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}
is well defined and provide explicit descriptions and estimates of this number. Unlike the symmetric version
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{(1,\ldots,1)}
considered by Severi and Lazarsfeld, the ordered intersection multiplicity in general depends on the ordering of
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
, and corresponds to a “step-by-step expansion of the intersection product
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})
from left to right” (
5.7
). However, in the case that
Z
is an
isolated
point of
V:=\bigcap_{i}\supp(F_{i})
, the ordered intersection multiplicity is independent of the ordering, and equals the usual intersection multiplicity of the
F_{i}
at
Z
. It follows that

(1)

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{X}^{iso}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n})-\sum_{Z\subseteq V_{+}}(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}

where
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{X}^{iso}
is the sum of intersection multiplicities of
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
over all isolated points of
V
, and
V_{+}
is the union of the positive dimensional irreducible components of
V
. Identity (
1
) opens the passage to applications of this ordered intersection multiplicity to the affine Bézout problem.

Example 2.2
.

We start with a classical example, considered e.g. in
[
Laz81
]
. Let
F_{1}=V(x_{0}^{2}x_{1})
,
F_{2}=V(x_{0}x_{1}^{2})\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and take
\mathcal{L}_{1}=\mathcal{L}_{2}=\mathcal{O}(3)
, the linear system on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
cut out by the cubics. Take
U:=\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus V(x_{2})
, and affine coordinates
(u_{0},u_{1}):=(x_{0}/x_{2},x_{1}/x_{2})
on
U
. Then
F_{i}=V(f_{i})
on
U
, where
f_{1}:=u_{0}^{2}u_{1}
and
f_{2}:=u_{0}u_{1}^{2}
. Let
\phi_{i}:=f_{i}-t^{r_{i}}g_{i}
, where
g_{1},g_{2}
are cubic polynomials in
(u_{0},u_{1})
. First we consider the case that
r_{1}=r_{2}=1
. Then

\displaystyle u_{1}\phi_{1}-u_{0}\phi_{2}=t(u_{0}g_{2}-u_{1}g_{1})

Write
C
for the (scheme-theoretic) closure of
C^{*}
. If
g_{1},g_{2}
are generic, then
C=V(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
is defined near
U\times\{0\}
by

\displaystyle f_{1}-tg_{1}

\displaystyle=u_{0}^{2}u_{1}-ta_{1}+\cdots,\ \text{and}

\displaystyle u_{0}g_{2}-u_{1}g_{1}

\displaystyle=u_{0}a_{2}-u_{1}a_{1}+\cdots

where the
a_{i}
are the constant terms of
g_{i}
(and “
\cdots
” denotes terms with higher order in
t
). In the generic case both
a_{i}
are nonzero, which implies that
C
is nonsingular at the origin
O:=(0,0,0)
, and both
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
are parameters of
C
at
O
. It follows that

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2})_{V(x_{0},x_{1})}^{(1,1)}=\ord_{(0,0,0)}(t|_{C})=\ord_{(0,0,0)}(u_{0}^{2}u_{1}|_{C})=3

Now we consider
Z=V(x_{0})
. Then
Z\cap U=V(u_{0})
and the intersection of
C\cap\{t=0\}
and
(Z\cap U)\times\{0\}
is
V(f_{1}-tg_{1},u_{0}g_{2}-u_{1}g_{1},t,u_{0})=V(f_{1},u_{1}g_{1},t,u_{0})
, which set theoretically consists of the origin
O
and
V(g_{1},u_{0})\times\{0\}
. In the generic case the latter set consists of three generic points on
Z\times\{0\}
distinct from
O
, and it is not hard to see that at each of these points
C
is nonsingular and
t
is a parameter. Consequently,

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2})_{V(x_{0})}^{(1,1)}

\displaystyle=3,\ \text{and similarly,}

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2})_{V(x_{1})}^{(1,1)}

\displaystyle=3

Since
(F_{1},F_{2})=9
, it follows that

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2})

\displaystyle=(F_{1},F_{2})_{V(x_{0},x_{1})}^{(1,1)}+(F_{1},F_{2})_{V(x_{0})}^{(1,1)}+(F_{1},F_{2})_{V(x_{1})}^{(1,1)}

is a complete decomposition of
(F_{1},F_{2})
, and
(F_{1},F_{2})_{Z}^{(1,1)}=0
for all
Z
different from
V(x_{0}),V(x_{1})
or
V(x_{0},x_{1})
.

Example 2.3
.

We continue with the same set up as
Example

2.2
. If
r_{1}\gg r_{2}
, then we claim that
g_{2}(z)=0
for each
z\in(C\cap\{t=0\})\cap(U\times\{0\})
. Indeed,
f_{2}=u_{0}u_{1}^{2}\in\sqrt{u_{0}^{2}u_{1}\mathcal{O}_{z,C}}=\sqrt{f_{1}\mathcal{O}_{z,C}}
. Consequently, there is
k\geq 1
such that
f_{2}^{k}=f_{1}h
for some
h\in\mathcal{O}_{z,C}
(it is clear that
k=2
works for this example), and

\displaystyle t^{kr_{2}}(g_{2})^{k}=(f_{2})^{k}=f_{1}h=ht^{r_{1}}g_{1}\in\mathcal{O}_{z,C}

Consequently, if
r_{1}>kr_{2}
, then
(g_{2})^{k}
vanishes at
z
, as claimed. Since a generic
g_{2}
does
not
vanish at the origin, it follows that

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2})_{V(x_{0},x_{1})}^{\vec{r}}=0

Since a generic
g_{2}
vanishes at
3
generic points on
V(x_{0})\cap U=V(u_{0})
, and since
\ord_{u_{0}}(f_{1})=2
, it can be shown that (see e.g. assertion (
2a
) of
7.4
)

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2})_{V(x_{0})}^{\vec{r}}

\displaystyle=2\times 3=6.

Similarly, since
\ord_{u_{1}}(f_{1})=1
, we have

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2})_{V(x_{1})}^{\vec{r}}

\displaystyle=1\times 3=3.

Since
(F_{1},F_{2})=9=6+3
, it follows that if
r_{1}\gg r_{2}
, then

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2})_{Z}^{\vec{r}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}6&\text{if}\ Z=V(x_{0}),\\
3&\text{if}\ Z=V(x_{1}),\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
r_{2}\gg r_{1}
, then it similarly follows that

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2})_{Z}^{\vec{r}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}3&\text{if}\ Z=V(x_{0}),\\
6&\text{if}\ Z=V(x_{1}),\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Example 2.4
.

Let
F_{2}=V(x_{0}x_{1}^{2})
be as above and
F^{\prime}_{1}=V(x_{0}^{2})
, i.e.
F^{\prime}_{1}
is a “component” of
F_{1}
from above. The same arguments as the previous example show that if
r_{1}\gg r_{2}
, then

\displaystyle(F^{\prime}_{1},F_{2})_{Z}^{\vec{r}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}6&\text{if}\ Z=V(x_{0}),\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

whereas, if
r_{2}\gg r_{1}
, then

\displaystyle(F^{\prime}_{1},F_{2})_{Z}^{\vec{r}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2&\text{if}\ Z=V(x_{0}),\\
4&\text{if}\ Z=V(x_{0},x_{1}),\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

2.5
.

We now describe the main results. Recall the basic set up: we are given fixed elements
F_{i}
of base-point free linear systems
\mathcal{L}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, on a variety
X
of dimension
n
. Note that we allow
F_{i}
to be identically zero on
X
. For generic
G_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{i}
, the subscheme of
X\times\mathbbm{k}
locally defined by
V(f_{1}-t^{r_{1}}g_{1},f_{n}-t^{r_{n}}g_{n})
, where
f_{i},g_{i}
are local representatives respectively of
F_{i}
and
G_{i}
, restricts to a (possibly non-reduced) curve
C^{*}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{n};r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})
on
X\times(\mathbbm{k}\setminus S)
, where
S
is a (possibly empty) finite subset of
\mathbbm{k}
; we write
C(G_{1},\ldots,G_{n};r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})
, or in short,
C(\vec{G};\vec{r})
, or simply
C
, for the scheme-theoretic closure of this curve in
X\times\mathbbm{k}
.

Theorem 2.6

(Ordered intersection multiplicity - definition)
.

Let
Z
be an irreducible subvariety of
X
. Then there is
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
and a proper closed subset
\check{Z}
of
Z
such that one (and only one) of the following holds:

(1)

for each
\vec{r}
such that
r_{i}/r_{i+1}>\nu
for each
i=1,\ldots,n-1
, there is a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{L}^{*}
of global sections of
\prod_{i}\mathcal{L}_{i}
such that
C
does not intersect
(Z\setminus\check{Z})\times\{0\}
for any
(G_{1},\ldots,G_{n})\in\mathcal{L}^{*}
,

(2)

or there is a positive integer
N_{Z}
depending only on
Z
such that for each
\vec{r}
such that
r_{i}/r_{i+1}>\nu
for each
i=1,\ldots,n-1
, and each nonempty open subset
Z^{*}
of
Z
, there is a nonempty open subset
\mathcal{L}^{*}
of
\prod_{i}\mathcal{L}_{i}
such that

(a)

C\cap((Z^{*}\setminus\check{Z})\times\{0\})\neq\emptyset
for each
(G_{1},\ldots,G_{n})\in\mathcal{L}^{*}
, and

(b)

the sum of
\ord_{B}(t)
over the branches
B
of
C
centered at
(Z^{*}\setminus\check{Z})\times\{0\}
is precisely
N_{Z}
.

We define
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}
to be zero in the first case, and equal to
N_{Z}
in the second case. There are finitely many irreducible subvarieties
Z
of
X
such that
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}
is nonzero. If
X
is complete, then

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n})=\sum_{Z}(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}

The subset
\check{Z}
can be described as follows:
\check{Z}
is the intersection of
Z
with the union of all irreducible subvarieties
Z^{\prime}\neq Z
of
X
such that
\codim(Z^{\prime})\geq\codim(Z)
and
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z^{\prime}}^{\ord}\neq 0
.

The subvarieties
Z
for which
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}
is nonzero can be explicitly described in terms of intersections of irreducible components of
\supp(F_{i})
. We found this surprising since descriptions of “distinguished varieties” tend to require passing to a “modification” of the original space (normal cone in Fulton-MacPherson theory
[
Ful98
]
, and a transcendental extension in Stückrad-Vogel theory
[
Vog84
]
).

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
i^{*}
be the smallest index such that
F_{i^{*}}
is not identically zero. For each collection of indices
i^{*}=i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\rho}\leq n
, where
1\leq\rho\leq n
, construct subvarieties
Z^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
,
0\leq j\leq\rho
, as follows:

(a)

Z^{(0)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}=X
,

(b)

for
1\leq j\leq\rho-1
,
Z^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
is the union of all irreducible components of
Z^{(j-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\cap\supp(F_{i_{j}})
which are not contained in
\bigcup_{j^{\prime}>j}\supp(F_{i_{j^{\prime}}})
, but are contained in
\supp(F_{i})
for each
i
,
i_{j}<i<i_{j+1}
,

(c)

Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
is the union of all irreducible components of
Z^{(\rho-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\cap\supp(F_{i_{\rho}})
which are contained in
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\supp(F_{i})
.

Then
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}>0
only if there are
i^{*}=i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\rho}\leq n
, where
\rho:=\codim(Z)
, such that
Z
is an irreducible component of some
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
.

Definition 2.8
.

By an
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho})
-type distinguished component
for (the ordered intersection of)
F_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak F_{n}
, we mean an irreducible component of some
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
from
2.7
. Note that it is possible for a distinguished component to have zero ordered intersection multiplicity - see
2.12
below.

Remark 2.9
.

If all
F_{i}
are identically zero, then the only distinguished component for
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
is
X
, and the number prescribed by
2.11
below for
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{X}^{\ord}
is precisely the intersection number
(\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n})
.

Remark 2.10
.

Every irreducible component
Z
of
\bigcap_{i}\supp(F_{i})
is a distinguished component for
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
. Indeed, if
\rho:=\codim(Z)=1
, then
i_{1}=i^{*}
works (where
i^{*}
is as in
2.7
). Otherwise if
\rho>1
, then set
i_{1}=i^{*}
and choose an irreducible component
H_{1}
of
V(f_{i^{*}})
containing
Z
, and let
i_{2}
be the smallest index such that
H_{1}
is not contained in any irreducible component of
V(f_{i_{2}})
. If
\rho=2
, then stop. Otherwise pick an irreducible component
H_{2}
of
H_{1}\cap V(f_{i_{2}})
containing
Z
, and pick the smallest
i_{3}
such that
H_{2}
is not contained in
V(f_{i_{3}})
. It is straightforward to check that this process can be continued up to
\rho
steps, and at the end we have
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}
as claimed.

For the distinguished components for (the ordered intersection of)
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
, we can give an explicit expression of
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}
in terms of the length of certain local rings.

Theorem 2.11
.

Let
Z
be an
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho})
-type distinguished component for
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
, where
\rho:=\codim(Z)
. Fix an open affine neighborhood
U
of
X
such that
U\cap Z\neq\emptyset
and each
F_{i}
is defined by a regular function
f_{i}
on
U
which is either identically zero (in the case that
F_{i}
is identically zero on
X
), or a non zero-divisor in
\mathbbm{k}[U]
. Consider ideals
I^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
,
0\leq j\leq\rho
, of
\mathbbm{k}[U]
defined as follows:

(a)

I^{(0)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
is the zero ideal,

(b)

I^{(1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
is the principal ideal generated by
f_{i_{1}}
,

(c)

for
2\leq j\leq\rho
,
I^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
is the ideal generated by
f_{i_{j}}
and the intersection of all primary components
\mathfrak{q}
of
I^{(j-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
such that the corresponding prime ideal
\sqrt{\mathfrak{q}}
contains
f_{i}
for each
i<i_{j}
, but does not contain
f_{i_{j}}
.

Then

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})\deg_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}(Z)

where the sum is over all
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}
such that
Z
is an
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho})
-type distinguished component for
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
, and
i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}
are the elements of
\{1,\ldots,n\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}\}
, and

\displaystyle\deg_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}(Z)

\displaystyle:=(\mathcal{L}_{i^{\prime}_{1}}|_{Z},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}|_{Z})

Corollary 2.12
.

Let
Z
be an irreducible subvariety of
X
. Then
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}>0
if and only if there are
i^{*}=i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\rho}\leq n
, where
\rho:=\codim(Z)
and
i^{*}
is the smallest index such that
F_{i^{*}}
is not identically zero, such that

(1)

Z
is an
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho})
-type distinguished component for
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
, and

(2)

\deg_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}(Z)>0
,

where
i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}
are the elements of
\{1,\ldots,n\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}\}
. ∎

Remark 2.13
.

\deg_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}(Z)
is the number (counted with appropriate multiplicities) of points in
Z\cap\bigcap_{j}\supp(G_{i^{\prime}_{j}})
for generic
G_{i^{\prime}_{j}}\in\mathcal{L}_{i^{\prime}_{j}}
. It is analogous to the usual degree of
Z
, and can be considered a “global factor” since it depends on the intersection theory of
X
and the line bundles
\mathcal{L}_{i}
. The term
\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})
is the “local factor” since it depends on how the
F_{i_{j}}
vanish along
Z
.

Remark 2.14
.

Let
Z
be a distinguished component for
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
. If
Z
is a point, then the “global factor” is
1
(since
\rho=n
, and therefore
\{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}\}
is the empty set), and if in addition
Z
is an isolated point of
\bigcap_{i}\supp(F_{i})
, then the “local factor”
\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})
is precisely the intersection multiplicity of
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
at
Z
(this follows e.g. since intersections of divisors are well defined as cycle classes on the support of the intersection
[
Ful98
, Theorem 2.4]
). On the other extreme, if
Z=X
(which is only possible if all the
F_{i}
are identically zero), then as stated in
2.9
, the local factor is
1
, and the global factor is
(\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n})
.

Remark 2.15
.

Let
F_{1}=V(x_{0}^{2}x_{1})
,
F_{2}=V(x_{0}x_{1}^{2})
,
F^{\prime}_{1}=V(x_{0}^{2})
be the divisors on
X:=\mathbb{P}^{2}
from
Examples

2.2
to
2.4
. It follows from the computations in
Examples

2.3
and
2.4
that

\displaystyle(F_{2},F_{1})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}3&\text{if}\ Z=V(x_{0}),\\
6&\text{if}\ Z=V(x_{1}),\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

\displaystyle(F_{2},F^{\prime}_{1})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2&\text{if}\ Z=V(x_{0}),\\
4&\text{if}\ Z=V(x_{0},x_{1}),\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Write
F^{\prime\prime}_{1}:=V(x_{1})
, so that
F_{1}=F^{\prime}_{1}+F^{\prime\prime}_{1}
. It can be shown similarly (e.g. using
Example

7.7
) that

\displaystyle(F_{2},F^{\prime\prime}_{1})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2&\text{if}\ Z=V(x_{1}),\\
1&\text{if}\ Z=V(x_{0},x_{1}),\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

In particular, it follows that in general
(F_{2},F_{1})_{Z}^{\ord}\neq(F_{2},F^{\prime}_{1})_{Z}^{\ord}+(F_{2},F^{\prime\prime}_{1})_{Z}^{\ord}
, even though
(F_{2},F_{1})=(F_{2},F^{\prime}_{1})+(F_{2},F^{\prime\prime}_{1})
.

2.16
.

2.6
,
2.7
and
2.11
are proved in
Part

I
of this article (i.e.
Sections

3
to
6
). In
Part

II
we explicitly compute the ordered intersection multiplicity in some special cases and present examples to illustrate applications to the affine Bézout problem of counting isolated solutions of systems of polynomials. In particular, we consider some criteria under which the following holds:

(property I)

the “local factor”
\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})
can be represented as an intersection multiplicity of hypersurfaces on a nonsingular variety at a proper isolated point of intersection.

If
X
is nonsingular, then (
property I
) holds e.g. when
\codim(Z)\leq 2
or
\codim(Z)=n
, or when
Z
is an isolated component of
\prod_{j=1}^{\rho}\supp(F_{i_{j}})
(
7.4
). In case (
property I
) holds, one can estimate the intersection multiplicity in terms of corresponding Newton diagrams and can also explicitly identify when this estimate is not exact using the results from
[
Mon21
, Chapter IX]
- this is described in
Sections

8.1
and
8.2
. In
Section

8.3
we describe a scenario under which a similar convex geometric estimate for the local factor is available even when (
property I
) does not hold. To illustrate an application of all these estimates to the affine Bézout problem, in
Section

9
we compute the number of common tangent lines to
4
general spheres in
\mathbbm{k}^{3}
by setting it as a counting problem on the Grassmannian of lines in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
(or equivalently, planes in
\mathbbm{k}^{4}
), and estimating the ordered intersection multiplicity of corresponding excess intersections
2
2

2

This problem was solved by Macdonald, Pach, and Theobald [Discrete Comput. Geom. 26 (1), 2001] via modelling it as a different counting problem without any excess intersections. Our exposition was inspired by a paragraph by Sottile and Theobald in
[
ST02
]
which mentions a similar explanation in terms of usual excess intersection multiplicities in a manuscript by Aluffi and Fulton.
. Finally,
Section

10
presents a few inductive “solutions” to the affine Bézout problem mentioned in
Section

1.2
.

Part I
Ordered intersection multiplicity

3.
Initial observations

3.1
.

In this section we make a few elementary observations which are the basis of all our results on ordered intersection multiplicity. We work locally on an irreducible affine variety
X
with dimension
n
and fixed regular functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\eta}
(in this section
\eta
can be an arbitrary positive integer, i.e. does not have to equal
n
). Given
g_{i}\in\mathbbm{k}[X]
and positive integers
r_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq\eta
, let

\displaystyle C^{*}(g_{1},\ldots,g_{\eta};r_{1},\ldots,r_{\eta})

\displaystyle:=V(f_{1}-t^{r_{1}}g_{1},\ldots,f_{\eta}-t^{r_{\eta}}g_{\eta})\cap(X\times(\mathbbm{k}\setminus\{0\}))

and let
C(g_{1},\ldots,g_{\eta};r_{1},\ldots,r_{\eta})
be the closure of
C^{*}(g_{1},\ldots,g_{\eta};r_{1},\ldots,r_{\eta})
in
X\times\mathbbm{k}
. Note that in this section and the next we treat these objects only as algebraic
varieties
, not schemes (in
Section

6
we would treat these as schemes), and often denote them simply by
C^{*}
and
C
if the
g_{i}
and
r_{j}
are clear from the context. Given a subvariety
Z
of
X
, we will study under what conditions
C
intersect generic points of
Z
at
t=0
for generic
g_{i}
in the case that
r_{i}/r_{i+1}\gg 1
for each
i
. We start with a simple observation.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
B
be a “branch” (see
Section

3.2.1
for the definition) of
C^{*}(g_{1},\ldots,g_{\eta};r_{1},\ldots,r_{\eta})
centered at
(z,0)
for some
z\in X
. Then

\displaystyle\ord_{B}(f_{i})\geq r_{i}\ord_{B}(t)

for each
i
; moreover
\ord_{B}(f_{i})=r_{i}\ord_{B}(t)
if and only if
g_{i}(z)\neq 0
. ∎

3.2.1
.

Let
C
be a curve and
\pi:C^{\prime}\to C
be a desingularization of
C
. A
branch

B
of
C
is the germ of a point
z
in
C^{\prime}
. The
center
of
B
is
y:=\pi(z)\in C
; we also say that
B
is a
branch of
C
at
y
. The
order
at
B
of a regular function
f
on
C
, denoted
\ord_{B}(f)
, is the order of vanishing of
f
at
z
.

3.3
.

For
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
, we write (
condition

r

.

/
r

.
+

>
ν

) as a shorthand for the following condition:

(condition
r_{.}/r_{.+}>\nu
)

\displaystyle r_{i}>0\ \text{and}\ r_{i}/r_{i+1}>\nu,\ 1\leq i\leq\eta-1,\text{and}\ r_{\eta}>\nu

We say that “a certain property
P
is true under (
condition

r

.

/
r

.
+

>
>
1

)” to mean the following:

(condition
r_{.}/r_{.+}>>1
)

there is a real number
\nu
such that property
P
holds whenever (
condition

r

.

/
r

.
+

>
ν

) holds.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
Z
be an irreducible subvariety of
X
such that
\prod_{i=1}^{s}f_{k^{\prime}_{i}}\in\sqrt{\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\rangle\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}}
for

. Then there is a nonempty open subset

of

such that under (
condition

r

.

/
r

.
+

>
>
1

, then

for some

.

Proof.

Assume
(\prod_{i=1}^{s}f_{k^{\prime}_{i}})^{m}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}h_{j}f_{j}
with
h_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}
. Let
U
be an open subset of

such that

and each

is a regular function on

such that

\displaystyle r_{j}>sm\max\{r_{k^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,r_{k^{\prime}_{s}}\}

for each

. Let

where

,

. Pick

such that
\ord_{B}(f_{k^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}})=\max_{i=1}^{s}\ord_{B}(f_{k^{\prime}_{i}})
. Then

\displaystyle\ord_{B}(f_{k^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{s}\sum_{i=1}^{s}\ord_{B}(f_{k^{\prime}_{i}})=\frac{1}{sm}\ord_{B}((\prod_{i=1}^{s}f_{k^{\prime}_{i}})^{m})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{sm}\min_{j=1}^{k}\ord_{B}(f_{j})\geq\frac{1}{sm}\min_{j=1}^{k}r_{j}\ord_{B}(t)>r_{k^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\ord_{B}(t)

It follows that
g_{k^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}(z)=0
(

3.2
), as required.
∎

Fix a subvariety

Remark 3.6

Proposition 3.7

Let

3.7.1

Now we assume the proposition is not true. Then, since there are only finitely many possibilities for the collections of

\nu\geq\nu_{k}
, then

\displaystyle\ord_{B}(h_{k})>\frac{1}{\mu_{k}}r_{i_{k-1}}\ord_{B}(t)

where we set
i_{0}:=i^{*}
for consistency.

Now since
Y_{\rho}\supseteq Z
has pure codimension
\rho=\codim(Z)
, there is an open subset
U^{*}
of
U^{*}_{\rho}
such that
Y_{\rho}\cap U^{*}=Z\cap U^{*}\neq\emptyset
. Since
Z\subseteq V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\eta})\subseteq V(f_{i_{\rho}})
, it follows that
(f_{i_{\rho}})^{m_{\rho}}\in\langle h_{1},\ldots,h_{\rho}\rangle\mathbbm{k}[U^{*}]
for some
m_{\rho}\geq 1
. By assumption
(ii)
from
Section

3.7.1
, there are
z\in U^{*}\cap Z
and
g_{i},r_{j}
which satisfy (

⁢

0

(
ν
)

) for some
\nu>\max\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{\rho},m_{\rho}\mu_{1},\ldots,m_{\rho}\mu_{\rho}\}
. Then for each branch
B
of
C
centered at
(z,0)
,

\displaystyle\ord_{B}(f_{i_{\rho}})

\displaystyle\geq\min_{k=1}^{\rho}\frac{\ord_{B}(h_{k})}{m_{\rho}}\geq\min_{k=1}^{\rho}\frac{r_{i_{k-1}}}{m_{\rho}\mu_{k}}\ord_{B}(t)>r_{i_{\rho}}\ord_{B}(t)

which contradicts
3.6
and completes the proof of
3.7
.
∎

4.
Distinguished components of an ordered intersection

In this section we start the study of ordered intersection multiplicity. The main result of this section is
4.8
which shows that the property of “having zero (or nonzero) ordered intersection multiplicity” is well defined, and there can be at most finitely many irreducible subvarieties with nonzero ordered intersection multiplicity, and all of them must be of the form described in
2.7
. The arguments used in this section are elementary (but involved) except for one place in the proof of
Lemma

4.6
where de Jong’s theorem on alteration
[
dJ96
, Theorem 4.1]
is used
3
3

3

We believe the results of this article could have been proved without using de Jong’s theorem on alteration; however, that would have made the arguments much more involved.
.

4.1
.
Set up

We start with a set up which is a bit more general than that of
1.1
. We work locally on an irreducible affine variety
X
of dimension
n
with fixed regular functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
and subsets
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
of
\mathbbm{k}[X]
such that for each

,

(1)

L_{i}
is an
irreducible constructible
set of dimension
\geq 1
, and

(2)

L_{i}
is
base-point free
, i.e. for each
x\in X
, there is
g\in L_{i}
such that
g(x)\neq 0
.

Given

and positive integers
r_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, we consider

as in
Section

3
.
Building on

and
3.7
, the following result shows how the ordering of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
dictates the subvarieties
Z
of
X
for which
C=C(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n};\allowbreak r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})
may intersect
Z\times\{0\}
under (
condition

r

.

/
r

.
+

>
>
1

) in the case that
\eta=n
.

Theorem 4.2
.

Assume we are in the set up of
Section

4.1
. Let
Z
be a subvariety of
X
with
\rho:=\codim(Z)>0
. Then there are
\nu^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
, a nonempty Zariski open subset
Z^{*}
of

, and a nonempty Zariski open subset
L^{*}
of
\prod_{i}L_{i}
such that for each
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\in L^{*}
, one (and only one) of the following holds for every
\nu\geq\nu^{*}
:

(1)

either
C
does not intersect
Z^{*}\times\{0\}
under (
condition

r

.

/
r

.
+

>
ν

),

(2)

or there are

and
r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
such that (
condition

r

.

/
r

.
+

>
ν

) holds and
(z,0)\in C
. In this case there are
\rho
choices for
i
, say

, such that

(c)

, where

, as in

, is the smallest index such that

is not identically zero, and

(d)

if and only if

for some

.

Remark 4.2.1
.

Note that
4.2
leaves open the possibility that some
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\in L^{*}
satisfies the first assertion, whereas some other
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\in L^{*}
satisfies the second. Moreover, it does
not
give any control on whether any of these options holds “uniformly”.

below provides a uniform version.

Example 4.2.2
.

It is possible (when
1<\rho<n
) that the same

satisfies the second assertion of
4.2
for more than one choices of
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}
(see e.g. identity (

) in

).

4.2.3
.
Proof of
4.2

Let

be the subset of

The first scenario is precisely the first possibility stated in

Lemma 4.4
.

Fix
i^{*}=i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{\rho}\leq n
and an open subset

of

such that

. Let

be a collection of pairs

of

and

such that

(i)

⁢

0

(

∗

,

∗

) holds with

and

for each

(ii)

for each

.

Let

. Then

(1)

.

(2)

vanishes on

, then for each

⁢

0

(

μ
∗

,

∗

)

) with

.

Proof.

First we prove assertion (

such that
(h^{*}\prod_{j^{\prime}>j}f_{i_{j^{\prime}}})^{m}
is in the ideal of

generated by all

such that
(h,i)\in\mathcal{H}
for some

. If

of

which satisfies (

⁢

0

(

)

) for some

\displaystyle\ord_{B}(h^{*})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{m}(\min_{(h,i)\in\mathcal{H}}\ord_{B}(h)-m\sum_{j^{\prime}>j}\ord_{B}(f_{i_{j^{\prime}}}))\geq\frac{1}{m}(\min_{(h,i)\in\mathcal{H}}\ord_{B}(h)-m(\rho-j)\ord_{B}(f_{i_{j+1}}))

Now, given

, as required to complete the proof of assertion (

4.5
.

Our next result shows that any subvariety of
X
not listed in
2.7
has zero ordered intersection multiplicity. Let
Z
be an irreducible subvariety of
X
. Assume at least one
f_{i}
is not identically zero; as in
3.6
, let
i^{*}
be the smallest such
i
. Fix
i^{*}=i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\rho}\leq n
, where
\rho:=\codim(Z)
and define subvarieties
\Phi^{(0)}_{Z},\ldots,\Phi^{(\rho)}_{Z}
of
X
as follows:

(a)

\Phi^{(0)}_{Z}:=X
,

(b)

for each
j=1,\ldots,\rho-1
,
\Phi^{(j)}_{Z}
is the union of all irreducible components of
\Phi^{(j-1)}_{Z}\cap V(f_{i_{j}})
containing
Z
which are not contained in
\bigcup_{j^{\prime}=j+1}^{\rho}V(f_{i_{j^{\prime}}})
, but are contained in
V(f_{i})
for each
i
,
i_{j}<i<i_{j+1}
.

(c)

for
j=\rho
,

\displaystyle\Phi^{(\rho)}_{Z}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}Z&\text{if}\ \Phi^{(\rho-1)}_{Z}\neq\emptyset\ \text{and}\ Z\subseteq V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}),\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Lemma 4.6
.

If
\Phi^{(j)}_{Z}=\emptyset
for some
j=1,\ldots,\rho
, then there is
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
, a nonempty open subset
Z^{*}
of
Z
, and a nonempty Zariski open subset
L^{*}
of
\prod_{i}L_{i}
such that no branch
B
of
C=C(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n};r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})
centered at
Z^{*}\times\{0\}
satisfies (

⁢

0

(
ν
)

) for any
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\in L^{*}
. In other words, for each
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\in L^{*}
, and each choice of positive
r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}
such that
r_{i}/r_{i+1}>\nu
,
1\leq i\leq n-1
, and each
z\in Z^{*}
such that
(z,0)\in C
, there is
j
,
1\leq j\leq\rho
, such that
g_{i_{j}}(z)=0
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma in several steps. First we reduce it to the case that the union of the set of zeroes of the
f_{i}
is the support of a
strict normal crossing divisor
4
4

4

An effective Cartier divisor
D
on a nonsingular variety
X
is a
strict normal crossing divisor
if near each
x\in\supp(D)
,
D
can be expressed as a sum of divisors of regular functions which collectively form a part of a regular system of parameters in
\mathcal{O}_{x,X}
.
using de Jong’s
alterations
5
5

5

We use the following weak version of de Jong’s theorem on “alterations”
[
dJ96
, Theorem 4.1]
: Let
Z
be a proper closed subset of a variety
X
. There is a surjective generically finite morphism
\pi:X^{\prime}\to X
such that
X^{\prime}
is nonsingular, and
\pi^{-1}(Z)
is the support of a strict normal crossing divisor on
X^{\prime}
.
[
dJ96
, Theorem 4.1]
.

Proposition 4.6.1
.

Let
\pi:X^{\prime}\to X
be a surjective morphism between varieties of the same dimension
n
. Assume
Lemma

4.6
holds for the ordered intersection of
V(\pi^{*}(f_{i}))
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, on
X^{\prime}
. Then
Lemma

4.6
holds for the ordered intersection of
V(f_{i})
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, on
X
.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the case that
Z\subseteq V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
, and
\Phi^{(j)}_{Z}
, as defined in
Lemma

4.6
, is empty for some
j=1,\ldots,\rho-1
, where
\rho:=\codim(Z)
.
Due to
4.2
then it suffices to show that there is no subvariety
Z^{\prime}
of
\pi^{-1}(Z)
such that

(i)

the codimension of
Z^{\prime}
in
X^{\prime}
equals the codimension
\rho
of
Z
in
X
,

(ii)

\pi(Z^{\prime})=Z
,

(iii)

\Phi^{(j)}_{Z^{\prime}}
, as defined in
Lemma

4.6
, are nonempty for each
j=1,\ldots,\rho
.

Assume to the contrary that there is a subvariety
Z^{\prime}
of
\pi^{-1}(Z)
satisfying the above properties. Note the following (obvious) observation:

(iv)

given a subset
W^{\prime}
of
X^{\prime}
,
W^{\prime}\subseteq V(\pi^{*}(f_{i}))
if and only if
\pi(W^{\prime})\subseteq V(f_{i})
. In particular, if
W^{\prime}
is irreducible and
W^{\prime}\not\subseteq V(\pi^{*}(f_{i}))
, and
\dim(W^{\prime})>\dim(\pi(W^{\prime}))
, then

(a)

either
W^{\prime}\cap V(\pi^{*}(f_{i}))=\emptyset
,

(b)

or
\dim(\pi(W^{\prime})\cap V(f_{i}))=\dim(\pi(W^{\prime}))-1<\dim(W^{\prime})-1=\dim(W^{\prime}\cap V(\pi^{*}(f_{i})))
.

It follows from the definition of
\Phi^{(\cdot)}_{\cdot}
and applications of the preceding observation that for each
j=1,\ldots,\rho
,

(v)

\pi(\Phi^{(j)}_{Z^{\prime}})
is the union of
\Phi^{(j)}_{Z}
and a (possibly empty) subvariety
W_{j}
of codimension
>j
in
X
.

Since
\Phi^{(\rho-1)}_{Z}=\emptyset
by assumption, it follows that
\pi(\Phi^{(\rho-1)}_{Z^{\prime}})=Z\subseteq V(f_{i_{\rho}})
. However, then observation
(iv)
implies that
\Phi^{(\rho-1)}_{Z^{\prime}}\subseteq V(\pi^{*}(f_{i_{\rho}}))
. This contradicts the definition of
\Phi^{(\rho-1)}_{\cdot}
, and therefore, proves
4.6.1
.
∎

4.6.2
.

We go back to the proof of
Lemma

4.6
. It suffices to consider the case that

(i)

Z\subseteq V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
, for otherwise
Lemma

4.6
is obvious with
Z^{*}:=Z\setminus V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
,

(ii)

\rho:=\codim(Z)>1
, since
Lemma

4.6
is clearly true if
\rho\leq 1
,

(iii)

and
\bigcup_{i}V(f_{i})
with the reduced scheme structure is a strict normal crossing divisor (due to
4.6.1
).

We will prove the contrapositive of
Lemma

4.6
. In particular, we assume that

(iv)

for each
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
, each nonempty open subset
Z^{*}
of
Z
, and each nonempty Zariski open subset
L^{*}
of
\prod_{i}L_{i}
, there are
r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
,
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\in L^{*}
, and a branch

of

centered at

such that (

⁢

0

(
ν
)

) holds.

Under these assumptions, we will show that
\Phi^{(j)}_{Z}
is nonempty for each

.

4.6.3
.

By assumption
(iii)
, there is an open subset

of

with local system of coordinates

such that

and each

which is not identically zero is an invertible function on

multiplied by a monomial in the

. Define subvarieties

of

containing

,

, as follows:

(a)

\Phi^{\prime(0)}_{Z}:=X
,

(b)

for each

,

is the union of all irreducible components of
\Phi^{\prime(j-1)}_{Z}\cap V(f_{i_{j}})
containing

which are not contained in
\bigcup_{j^{\prime}=j+1}^{\rho}V(f_{i_{j^{\prime}}})
.

Claim 4.6.4
.

Under the assumptions of
Section

4.6.2
,
\Phi^{\prime(j)}_{Z}\neq\emptyset
,
j=0,\ldots,\rho
. There are indices
k_{1},\ldots,k_{\rho}
and a nonempty open subset

of

such that

(1)

,

(2)

for each

,

x_{k_{j}}

, but does not divide

for any

,

(b)

\Phi^{\prime(j)}_{Z}\cap U^{\prime}\supseteq V(x_{k_{1}},\ldots,x_{k_{j}})\cap U^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
,

(3)

In particular,
\Phi^{\prime(\rho)}_{Z}\cap U^{\prime}=V(x_{k_{1}},\ldots,x_{k_{\rho}})\cap U^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Write
f_{i_{1}}=u_{i_{1}}\prod_{k}x_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}
, where

is invertible on

. Let

be the product of all

such that

does

for any

. Due to assumption

of

, we may apply

with

,

and

to obtain that property (

⁢

0

(

μ
∗

,

ν
∗

)

) is satisfied for all
\mu^{*},\nu^{*}
with

and

; in particular,
V(f^{\prime}_{i_{1}})\cap U\neq\emptyset
. Now assume we have inductively defined monomials

in the

such that

(i)

,

(ii)

each

divides
f_{i_{k}}
, and is relatively prime to
\prod_{j^{\prime}>k}f_{i_{j^{\prime}}}
,

(iii)

property (

⁢

0

(

μ
∗

,

ν
∗

)

) is satisfied for all
\mu^{*},\nu^{*}
with
\mathcal{H}^{*}=\{(f^{\prime}_{i_{1}},i_{2}),\ldots,(f^{\prime}_{i_{j}},i_{j+1})\}
and
Z^{*}=Z\cap U
.

If
j=\rho-1
, then we define
f^{\prime}_{i_{j+1}}:=f_{i_{\rho}}
, and stop. Otherwise, write
f_{i_{j+1}}=u_{i_{j+1}}\prod_{k}x_{k}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{k}}
, where
u_{i_{j+1}}
is invertible on

, and define
f^{\prime}_{i_{j+1}}
to be the product of all
x_{k}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{k}}
such that

does
not
divide

for any
j^{\prime}>j+1
. It is then straightforward to see by an application of

4.4
for

with
\mathcal{H}=\{(f^{\prime}_{i_{1}},i_{2}),\ldots,(f^{\prime}_{i_{j}},i_{j+1})\}
and

that the process can be continued to the next step with
f^{\prime}_{i_{j+1}}
. This implies in particular that
V(f^{\prime}_{i_{1}},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{i_{\rho}})
is a nonempty union of “coordinate subspaces” of codimension
\rho
, i.e. varieties of the form
x_{k_{1}}=\cdots=x_{k_{\rho}}=0
for
1\leq k_{1}<\cdots k_{\rho}\leq n
. Since
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is a local system of coordinates on
U
, and
Z
is irreducible of codimension
\rho
, there is an open subset
U^{\prime}
of
U
such that
U^{\prime}\cap Z
is nonempty and equals the intersection with
U^{\prime}
of precisely one such coordinate subspace. All assertions of the claim follow from this observation.
∎

Consider
k_{1},\ldots,k_{\rho}
from
4.6.4
. Pick
j
,
1\leq j\leq\rho-1
, and
i
such that
i_{j}<i<i_{j+1}
.

Claim 4.6.5
.

There is
j^{\prime}\leq j
such that
x_{k_{j^{\prime}}}
divides
f_{i}
.

Proof.

Write
f_{i}=u_{i}\prod_{k}x_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}
where
u_{i}
is invertible on
U
. Assume the claim does not hold, so that
\alpha_{k_{j^{\prime}}}=0
for all
j^{\prime}\leq j
. Since
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
form a local system of coordinates everywhere on
U
, it follows that
Z^{*}:=(Z\cap U)\setminus V(x_{k}:k\neq k_{j^{\prime}}
for any
j^{\prime})
is nonempty. If
r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}
and
B
satisfy property
(iv)
of
Section

4.6.2
for
Z^{*}
with
\nu\geq 1
, then

\displaystyle\ord_{B}(f_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j^{\prime}>j}\alpha_{k_{j^{\prime}}}\ord_{B}(x_{k_{j^{\prime}}})\leq\sum_{j^{\prime}>j}\alpha_{k_{j^{\prime}}}\ord_{B}(f_{i_{j^{\prime}}})\leq(\rho-j)(\max_{j^{\prime}=j+1}^{\rho}\alpha_{k_{j^{\prime}}})r_{i_{j+1}}\ord_{B}(t)

However, then
\ord_{B}(f_{i})<r_{i}\ord_{B}(t)
for
\nu\gg 1
, contradicting property (
condition

r

.

/
r

.
+

>
ν

). This proves the claim.
∎

If
U^{\prime}
is as in
4.6.4
, then
\Phi^{(j)}_{Z}\cap U^{\prime}\supseteq V(x_{k_{1}},\ldots,x_{k_{j}})\cap U^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
for each

(
4.6.4
and

). This completes the proof of

4.6
.
∎

4.7
.

We now show how
Lemma

4.6
implies that the “ordered intersection multiplicity” can be nonzero for at most finitely many subvarieties of
X
. As in
3.6
, let
i^{*}
be the smallest index such that
f_{i^{*}}
is not identically zero. For each
i^{*}=i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\rho}\leq n
, where
1\leq\rho\leq n:=\dim(X)
, let
Z^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
,
0\leq j\leq\rho
, be as in
2.7
, i.e.

(a)

Z^{(0)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}=X
,

(b)

for
1\leq j\leq\rho-1
,
Z^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
is the union of all irreducible components of
Z^{(j-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\cap\supp(F_{i_{j}})
which are not contained in
\bigcup_{j^{\prime}>j}\supp(F_{i_{j^{\prime}}})
, but are contained in
\supp(F_{i})
for each
i
,
i_{j}<i<i_{j+1}
,

(c)

Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
is the union of all irreducible components of
Z^{(\rho-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\cap\supp(F_{i_{\rho}})
which are contained in
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\supp(F_{i})
.

We write
\mathcal{Z}^{(\rho)}
for the union of
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
over all choices of
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}
.

Theorem 4.8
.

Assume we are in the set up of

.

(1)

Remark 4.8.1
.

In the case that

) of

, we say that the “ordered intersection multiplicity of

at

4.8.2

Proof of assertion (

) of

The second assertion of

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{(\rho,*)}_{k}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\mathcal{Z}^{(\rho)}_{k}\setminus Z&\text{if}\ \mathcal{Z}^{(\rho)}_{k}\supsetneq Z,\\

Then assertion (

such that

.

5.
Combinatorics of intersections of divisors - guessing the formula for the ordered intersection multiplicity

5.1
.

Consider effective Cartier divisors
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
on a variety
X
of pure dimension
n
. We allow for the scenario that
\bigcap\supp(F_{i})
has nonzero dimension, and try to express the intersection product
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})
as a sum of contributions from the “distinguished components”, i.e. the irreducible components of
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
from
2.7
. Recall from the definition of the
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
that for all such components,
i_{1}
takes a fixed value, which we denoted as
i^{*}
. Moreover,
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
is constructed via an inductive step starting from irreducible components of
\supp(F_{i_{1}})
which are contained in
\supp(F_{i_{2}})
, but
not
contained in
\supp(F_{i})
for any
i
,
i_{1}<i<i_{2}
; in other words, for each such component
Z
of
\supp(F_{i_{1}})
,
i_{2}
is the
smallest
value of
i
such that
Z\not\subseteq\supp(F_{i})
.

5.2
.

Motivated by the observations in the preceding paragraph, we do the following:

(i)

Fix
i_{1}:=i^{*}
,
1\leq i^{*}\leq n
. Denote the irreducible components of
F_{i_{1}}
as
F_{i_{1};j}
, so that as a Weil divisor
F_{i_{1}}
is of the form
\sum_{j}m_{i_{1};j}F_{i_{1};j}
.

(ii)

For each
j
we consider the smallest index
k_{j}>i_{1}
such that
F_{i_{1};j}\not\subseteq\supp(F_{k_{j}})

Then

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})

\displaystyle=(F_{i_{1}},\ldots,F_{n},F_{1},\ldots,F_{i_{1}-1})=(\sum_{j}m_{i_{1};j}F_{i_{1};j},F_{2},\ldots,F_{n},F_{1},\ldots,F_{i_{1}-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}m_{i_{1};j}(F_{i_{1};j},F_{k_{j}},F_{k_{j}+1},\ldots,F_{n},F_{1},\ldots,F_{i_{1}-1},F_{2},\ldots,F_{k_{j}-1})

In line with the construction of the
Z^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
from
2.7
, we write
i_{2}=k_{j}
, and rearrange the terms by fixing first a value of
i_{2}
and summing over all
j
such that
k_{j}=i_{2}
to obtain

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{2}}\sum_{k_{j}=i_{2}}m_{1;j}(F_{i_{1};j},F_{i_{2}},F_{i_{2}+1},\ldots,F_{n},F_{1},\ldots,F_{i_{1}-1},F_{i_{1}+1},\ldots,F_{i_{2}-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{2}}(F_{i_{1};\Sigma i_{2}},F_{i_{2}},F_{i_{2}+1},\ldots,F_{n},F_{1},\ldots,F_{i_{1}-1},F_{i_{1}+1},\ldots,F_{i_{2}-1})

where
F_{i_{1};\Sigma i_{2}}
is the Weil divisor
\sum_{j:k_{j}=i_{2}}m_{1;j}F_{i_{1};j}
.

5.3
.

Note that each irreducible component of
\supp(F_{i_{1};\Sigma i_{2}})\cap\supp(F_{i_{2}})
has codimension two, so that the intersection

\displaystyle F_{i_{1},i_{2}}:=F_{i_{1};\Sigma i_{2}}\cap F_{i_{2}}

can be represented as a codimension two cycle
\sum_{j}m_{i_{1},i_{2};j}F_{i_{1},i_{2};j}
consisting of linear combination of irreducible components of
\supp(F_{i_{1};\Sigma i_{2}})\cap\supp(F_{i_{2}})
. Then

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{2}}(F_{i_{1},i_{2}},F_{i_{2}+1},\ldots,F_{n},F_{1},\ldots,F_{i_{1}-1},F_{i_{1}+1},\ldots,F_{i_{2}-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{2},j}m_{i_{1},i_{2};j}(F_{i_{1},i_{2};j},F_{i_{2}+1},\ldots,F_{n},F_{1},\ldots,F_{i_{1}-1},F_{i_{1}+1},\ldots,F_{i_{2}-1})

We now apply the same procedure as in
Section

5.2
: for each
j
, write

\displaystyle(F_{i_{1},i_{2};j},F_{i_{2}+1},\ldots,F_{n},F_{1},\ldots,F_{i_{1}-1},F_{i_{1}+1},\ldots,F_{i_{2}-1})

\displaystyle\quad\qquad=(F_{i_{1},i_{2};j},F_{k^{\prime}_{j}},F_{k^{\prime}_{j}+1},\ldots,F_{n},F_{1},\ldots,F_{i_{1}-1},F_{i_{1}+1},\ldots,F_{i_{2}-1},F_{i_{2}+1},\ldots,F_{k^{\prime}_{j}-1})

where
k^{\prime}_{j}
is the smallest index such that
F_{i_{1},i_{2};j}\not\subseteq\supp(F_{k^{\prime}_{j}})
. Then fix first a value of
i_{3}
and sum over all
j
such that
k^{\prime}_{j}=i_{3}
to obtain

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{i_{2},i_{3}}(F_{i_{1},i_{2};\Sigma i_{3}},F_{i_{3}},F_{i_{3}+1},\ldots,F_{n},F_{1},\ldots,F_{i_{1}-1},F_{i_{1}+1},\ldots,F_{i_{2}-1},F_{i_{2}+1},\ldots,F_{i_{3}-1})

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{i_{2},i_{3}}(F_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}},F_{i_{3}+1},\ldots,F_{n},F_{1},\ldots,F_{i_{1}-1},F_{i_{1}+1},\ldots,F_{i_{2}-1},F_{i_{2}+1},\ldots,F_{i_{3}-1})

where

\displaystyle F_{i_{1},i_{2};\Sigma i_{3}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k^{\prime}_{j}=i_{3}}m_{i_{1},i_{2};j}F_{i_{1},i_{2};j},\ \text{and}

\displaystyle F_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}

\displaystyle:=F_{i_{1},i_{2};\Sigma i_{3}}\cap F_{i_{3}}

5.4
.

Repeating this process as many times as possible we obtain

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rho=1}^{n}\sum_{i^{*}=i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\rho}\leq n}(F_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}},F_{i^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,F_{i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rho=1}^{n}\sum_{i^{*}=i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\rho}\leq n}\sum_{j}m_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}(F_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j},F_{i^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,F_{i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}})

where
1\leq i^{\prime}_{1}<\cdots<i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}\leq n
are the elements of
\{1,\ldots,n\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}\}
. It follows from the construction of the
F_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}
that for each
j
,

(i)

F_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}\subseteq\bigcap_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n-\rho}\supp(F_{i^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}})
for each
j
, and

(ii)

\codim(F_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j})=\rho
.

Due to observation
(ii)
, we can rearrange the terms of the above sum to obtain

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{Z}\sum_{F_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}=Z}m_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}\deg_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}(Z)

where
\deg_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}(Z):=(\mathcal{L}_{i^{\prime}_{1}}|_{Z},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}|_{Z})
, the outer sum is over all irreducible subvarieties
Z
of
X
, and the inner sum is over all
\rho,i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho},j
such that
\rho=\codim(Z)
and
Z=F_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}
.

5.5
.

We claim that
F_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}
from
Section

5.4
are precisely the irreducible components of
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
from
2.7
. Indeed, recall the definition of
Z^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
:

(a)

Z^{(0)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}=X
,

(b)

for
1\leq j\leq\rho-1
,
Z^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
is the union of all irreducible components of
Z^{(j-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\cap\supp(F_{i_{j}})
which are not contained in
\bigcup_{j^{\prime}>j}\supp(F_{i_{j^{\prime}}})
, but are contained in
\supp(F_{i})
for each
i
,
i_{j}<i<i_{j+1}
,

(c)

Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
is the union of all irreducible components of
Z^{(\rho-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\cap\supp(F_{i_{\rho}})
which are contained in
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\supp(F_{i})
.

It is clear from the definition that
\supp(F_{i_{1};\Sigma i_{2}})
is the union of
Z^{(1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
and a (possibly empty) collection of irreducible components of
\supp(F_{i_{1}})
which are contained in
\bigcup_{j^{\prime}=2}^{\rho}\supp(F_{i_{j^{\prime}}})
. By an easy induction then it follows that
\supp(F_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1};\Sigma i_{k}})
is the union of
Z^{(k-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
and a (possibly empty) collection of irreducible components which are contained in
\bigcup_{j^{\prime}=k+1}^{\rho}\supp(F_{i_{j^{\prime}}})
. However, this means that
\supp(F_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho-1};\Sigma i_{\rho}})
is precisely
Z^{(\rho-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
. This in turn implies (due to observation
(i)
from
Section

5.4
) that
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
is precisely the union of the
F_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}
, as claimed.

5.6
.

Now we compute the numbers
m_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}
from
Section

5.4
. Take an affine open subset
U
of
X
such that
U
intersects
F_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}
, and each
F_{i}
is the divisor of some
f_{i}\in\mathbbm{k}[U]
. Recall the definition of
I^{(k)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
from
2.11
:

(a)

I^{(1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
is the principal ideal generated by
f_{i_{1}}
,

(b)

for
2\leq k\leq\rho
,
I^{(k)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
is the ideal generated by
f_{i_{k}}
and the intersection of all primary components
\mathfrak{q}
of
I^{(k-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
such that the corresponding prime ideal
\sqrt{\mathfrak{q}}
contains
f_{i}
for each
i<i_{j}
, but does
not
contain
f_{i_{k}}
.

It is straightforward to check from the definitions and
A.2
that for each
k\leq\rho
, the subscheme of
U
defined by
I^{(k)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
is precisely the cycle
F_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}
constructed via the inductive procedure in
Sections

5.2
to
5.4
. In particular,
m_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}
is precisely the multiplicity of
Z=F_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}
in
V(I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}})
. Consequently,

(2)

\displaystyle m_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}

\displaystyle=\len_{\mathcal{O}_{Z,V(I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}})}}(\mathcal{O}_{Z,V(I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}})})=\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})

We summarize the observations from
Sections

5.4
to
5.6
in the following theorem:

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
be effective Cartier divisors on a variety
X
of pure dimension
n
. Fix
i^{*}
,
1\leq i^{*}\leq n
. Then

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{Z}\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})\deg_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}(Z)

where the outer sum is over all irreducible subvarieties
Z
of
X
, the inner sum is over all
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}
where
\rho:=\codim(Z)
and
i^{*}=i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\rho}\leq n
are such that
Z
is an irreducible component of
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
from
Section

5.5
, the
I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
are as in
Section

5.6
, and finally,
1\leq i^{\prime}_{1}<\cdots<i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}\leq n
are the elements of
\{1,\ldots,n\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}\}
, and
\deg_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}(Z)
is the number (counted with appropriate multiplicities) of points in the intersection of
Z
and generic Cartier divisors linearly equivalent to
F_{i^{\prime}_{j}}
,
j=1,\ldots,n-\rho
. ∎

5.8
.

Based on
5.7
we may guess that the ordered intersection multiplicity of
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
along an irreducible subvariety
Z
equals

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})\deg_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}(Z)

where
\rho:=\codim(Z)
, and the sum is over all
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}
such that
Z
is an irreducible component of
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
. In the next section we show that this guess is indeed correct.

6.

The main result of this section is the computation of the formula of the ordered intersection multiplicity (

6.3
.

Given

\displaystyle\mapsto[\frac{f_{1}}{g_{1}}(x):\cdots:\frac{f_{n}}{g_{n}}(x):t]\in W

Given

\tilde{C}(\vec{ag})
be the ‘‘scheme-theoretic strict transform’’
7
7

7

The scheme-structure on

is given as follows: near all but finitely many points of

is locally isomorphic to

, so that the scheme-structure on

can be uniquely transferred to

of

on

. If

and

, where

is the natural projection and

are (weighted) homogeneous coordinates on

. Note that

,

, are well-defined regular functions on

.

Claim 6.3.1
.

Consider the set up in

6.1
and

. Pick

and

.

/
r

.
+

>
ν

), and

\displaystyle\len(\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C(\vec{ag})}/t\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C(\vec{ag})})

\displaystyle=\sum_{E\in\tilde{}\mathcal{E}_{0}}\ord_{E}(t)\deg(\pi_{W}|_{E})

Proof.

Since

is open (and nonempty), there is a nonempty open subset

of

\vec{ag}\in L^{*}_{1}

6.3.2
.

Let
\nu,r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},\vec{g},\mathcal{A}^{*}
be as in

. For a positive integer

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{*}_{m}:=\{\vec{a}\in\mathcal{A}^{*}:~

\displaystyle\vec{\mu a}:=(\mu_{1}a_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}a_{n})\in\mathcal{A}^{*}\ \text{for all}\ \vec{\mu}:=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})

\displaystyle\text{such that}\ \mu_{1}^{m}=\cdots=\mu_{n}^{m}=1\}

Then

is a nonempty Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{A}^{*}
. Pick

and

Corollary 6.3.3
.

Let
C_{m,i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}}(\vec{ag};\vec{r})
(or in short,
C_{m,i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}}(\vec{ag})
) be the scheme-theoretic closure in
X\times\mathbbm{k}
of the subscheme of
X\times(\mathbbm{k}\setminus\{0\})
defined by the following equations:

\displaystyle f_{i_{j}}-a_{i_{j}}t^{r_{i_{j}}}g_{i_{j}}=0,\quad

\displaystyle j=1,\ldots,\rho^{\prime},

\displaystyle(f_{i^{\prime}_{j}})^{m}-(a_{i^{\prime}_{j}})^{m}t^{mr_{i^{\prime}_{j}}}(g_{i^{\prime}_{j}})^{m}=0,\quad

\displaystyle j=1,\ldots,n-\rho^{\prime},

where
i^{\prime}_{1}<\cdots<i^{\prime}_{n-\rho^{\prime}}
are the elements of
\{1,\ldots,n\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}\}
. Then

\displaystyle\len(\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C(\vec{ag})}/t\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C(\vec{ag})})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m^{n-\rho^{\prime}}}\len(\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C_{m,i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}}(\vec{ag})}/t\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C_{m,i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}}(\vec{ag})})

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}}
be the set of all
(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})\in(\mathbbm{k}\setminus\{0\})^{n}
such that
\mu_{1}^{m}=\cdots=\mu_{n}^{m}=1
, and in addition,
\mu_{i_{j}}=1
,
j=1,\ldots,\rho^{\prime}
. Since
|\mathcal{M}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}}|=m^{n-\rho^{\prime}}
,
6.3.1
implies that

\displaystyle\len(\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C(\vec{ag})}/t\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C(\vec{ag})})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m^{n-\rho^{\prime}}}\sum_{\vec{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}}}\len(\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C(\vec{\mu ag})}/t\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C(\vec{\mu ag})})

The right hand side of the identity above equals
\frac{1}{{m^{n-\rho^{\prime}}}}\len(\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C_{m,i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}}(\vec{ag})}/t\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C_{m,i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}}(\vec{ag})})
due to the additivity of the “order” function of a non zero-divisor over branches of a curve (see e.g.
[
Mon21
, Theorem IV.24]
).
∎

Proposition 6.3.4
.

Let
Z^{\prime},i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}
be such that
Z^{\prime}
is an irreducible component of
Z^{(\rho^{\prime})}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}}
, and let
r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}
be as in
6.3.1
. Then there is a nonempty open subset
L^{*}
of
\prod_{i}L_{i}
such that the following sum is constant for each

:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in Z^{\prime}\\
\mathcal{Z}^{(\rho_{z})}_{k_{z}}=Z^{\prime}\\
(i_{1}(z),\ldots,i_{\rho_{z}}(z))=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}})\end{subarray}}}\len(\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C(\vec{g})}/t\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C(\vec{g})})

Proof.

Let
\nu,r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}
and
W:=\mathbb{P}^{n}(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},1)
be as in
6.3.1
, and let
Y
be the closure in

of the graph of the map

\displaystyle\prod_{i}L_{i}\times\bar{X}\times\mathbbm{k}\ni(\vec{g},x,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto[\frac{f_{1}}{g_{1}}(x):\cdots:\frac{f_{n}}{g_{n}}(x):t]\in W

Fix
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}
such that
Z^{\prime}
is an irreducible component of
Z^{(\rho^{\prime})}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}}
, and consider

\displaystyle Y_{0}

\displaystyle:=\{(\vec{g},x,t)\in Y:x\in Z,\ g_{i^{\prime}_{1}}(x)=\cdots=g_{i^{\prime}_{n-\rho^{\prime}}}(x)=t=0\},

where the
i^{\prime}_{j}
are the elements of
\{1,\ldots,n\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}\}
. Note that
Y_{0}
is a (possibly reducible) closed subvariety of
Y
. Let
[w_{1}:\cdots:w_{n+1}]
be (weighted) homogeneous coordinates on
W
, and
u_{i}:=w_{i}/(w_{n+1})^{r_{i}}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, be the coordinates on
U_{n+1}:=W\setminus V(w_{n+1})
. Let

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{0}

\displaystyle:=Y_{0}\cap V(u_{1}-1,\ldots,u_{n}-1)\cap\pi_{W}^{-1}(U_{n+1})

where
\pi_{W}:Y\to W
is the natural projection. Let
\pi_{L}:Y\to\prod_{i}L_{i}
is the natural projection. If
\pi_{L}|_{\tilde{L}_{0}}
is
not
dominant, then
6.3.4
holds with the required sum equal to zero. So assume
\pi_{L}|_{\tilde{L}_{0}}
is dominant. Then the arguments from the proof of
6.3.1
imply that

(i)

there is a nonempty open subset
L^{*}_{0}
of
\prod_{i}L_{i}
over which each fiber of
\pi_{L}|_{\tilde{L}_{0}}
is zero dimensional, and each fiber of
\pi_{L}|_{Y_{0}}
has pure dimension
n
.

In particular,
Y_{0}
has codimension one in
Y
, and consequently, some irreducible components of
Y_{0}
are also irreducible components of
V(t)\subseteq Y
. The observations from the proof of
6.3.1
then imply that

(ii)

there is a nonempty open subset
L^{*}_{1}
of
L^{*}_{0}
such that on
\tilde{U}^{1}_{n+1}:=\pi_{W}^{-1}(U_{n+1})\cap\pi_{L}^{-1}(L^{*}_{1})
, we can consider
\tilde{L}_{0}
as the (restriction of the) closed subscheme
V(t,u_{1}-1,\ldots,u_{n}-1)
.

(iii)

Denote the irreducible components of
\tilde{L}_{0}\cap\tilde{U}^{1}_{n+1}
by
\tilde{L}_{0,i}
. Then for each
\vec{g}
in a nonempty open subset
L^{*}_{2}
of
L^{*}_{1}
, the “scheme-theoretic strict transform” (see
Footnote

7
on
Footnote

7
)
\tilde{C}(\vec{g})
of
C(\vec{g})
on
Y
is the same as the subscheme
V(u_{1}-1,\ldots,u_{n}-1)
near
\{\vec{g}\}\times Z\times\{0\}
, and

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in Z^{\prime}\\
\mathcal{Z}^{(\rho_{z})}_{k_{z}}=Z^{\prime}\\
(i_{1}(z),\ldots,i_{\rho_{z}}(z))=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}})\end{subarray}}\hskip-30.00005pt\len(\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C(\vec{g})}/t\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C(\vec{g})})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\len(\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{L}_{0,i},\tilde{L}_{0}})\deg(\pi_{L}|_{\tilde{L}_{0,i}})

Since the right hand side does
not
depend on
\vec{g}\in L^{*}_{2}
, this completes the proof of
6.3.4
with
L^{*}=L^{*}_{2}
.
∎

The following theorem is the main result of this section. It confirms that the formula for the ordered intersection multiplicity guessed in

5.8
is the correct one. For this result
X
is an

), and

be the intersection of

with the union of all other distinguished components

with

.

Proposition 6.4.8
.

i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}

is from
Section

6.4.5
). Now for each
m
, let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime*}_{m,\vec{r}_{m}}(\vec{g})
be the nonempty open subset of
(\mathbbm{k}\setminus\{0\})^{n}
consisting of all
\vec{a}\in\mathcal{A}^{*}_{m,\vec{r}_{m}}(\vec{g})
such that
\vec{ag}\in L^{*}_{\nu_{m},\vec{r}_{m}}
. The uncountability of
\mathbbm{k}
again implies that there is
\vec{a}\in\bigcap_{m}\mathcal{A}^{\prime*}_{m,\vec{r}_{m}}(\vec{g})
, which yields a contradiction via
6.4.7
.
∎

6.4.9
.

Combining the observations from
Section

6.4.5
,
6.4.8
and
5.7
we obtain that for each
\vec{r}
satisfying (
condition

r

.

/
r

.
+

>
ν

) with sufficiently large
\nu
, there is a nonempty open subset
L^{*}
of
\prod_{i}L_{i}
such that for all
\vec{g}\in L^{*}
,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{Z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}\\
\rho^{\prime}=\codim(Z^{\prime})\\
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z^{(\rho^{\prime})}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in Z^{\prime}\\
\mathcal{Z}^{(\rho_{z})}_{k_{z}}=Z^{\prime}\\
(i_{1}(z),\ldots,i_{\rho_{z}}(z))=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}})\end{subarray}}\hskip-30.00005pt\len(\mathcal{O}_{(z,0)),C(\vec{g};\vec{r})}/t\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C(\vec{g};\vec{r})})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{Z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}\\
\rho^{\prime}=\codim(Z^{\prime})\\
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z^{(\rho^{\prime})}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}}\end{subarray}}\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z^{\prime},\bar{X}}/I^{(\rho^{\prime})}_{\rho^{\prime},k;i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}}}\mathcal{O}_{Z^{\prime},\bar{X}})\deg_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}(Z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n})

Then the inequality in the middle must be an equality, and for each

and each

we must have

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in Z^{\prime}\\
\mathcal{Z}^{(\rho_{z})}_{k_{z}}=Z^{\prime}\\
(i_{1}(z),\ldots,i_{\rho_{z}}(z))=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho^{\prime}})\end{subarray}}}\len(\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C(\vec{g};\vec{r})}/t\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C(\vec{g};\vec{r})})

\displaystyle=\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z^{\prime},\bar{X}}/I^{(\rho^{\prime})}_{\rho^{\prime},k;i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z^{\prime},\bar{X}})\deg_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}(Z^{\prime})

6.4
now follows from taking

of

in

.
