Part II
Computation: estimates and examples

7.
Local factors of ordered intersection as (usual) intersection multiplicities

7.1
.

Recall the basic set up: we are given base-point free linear systems
\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}
on a purely
n
-dimensional variety
X
, and for each
i
, we have a fixed element
F_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{i}
, which is either an effective (Cartier) divisor, or the zero element in
\mathcal{L}_{i}
.
5.7
shows that
9
9

9

In
5.7
no
F_{i}
is allowed to be identically zero. If say
k
of the
F_{i}
are identically zero, then one obtains identities (
3
) and (
4
) as follows: first reorder the
F_{i}
such that all the identically zero elements appear first, and then appear the remaining ones, but with the
ordering among the non identically-zero elements unchanged
. Then replace the identically zero elements by generic elements of the corresponding
\mathcal{L}_{i}
, and apply
5.7
with
i^{*}=k+1
.

(3)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{Z}(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}

where
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}
is the “ordered intersection multiplicity” of
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
along
Z
, which is zero unless
Z
is a “distinguished component” of the ordered intersection of
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
, in which case

(4)

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})\deg_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}(Z)

where
\rho:=\codim(Z)
, and the sum is over all
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}
are such that
Z
is an irreducible component of
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
from
2.7
, and
I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
are as in
2.11
. In this section we describe a few cases where the “local factors”
\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})
can be interpreted as “intersection multiplicities of regular sequences” at nonsingular points, and work out basic examples in dimension
\leq 3
.

7.2
.

First we recall the relevant definitions:

•

Let
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}
be regular functions on an open subset
U
of
X
, and
W
be an irreducible component of
V(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})
. If
\codim(W)=k
, then we say that
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}
form a
regular sequence near

W
; if in addition
W
is not contained in the set
\sing(X)
of singular points of
X
, then the
intersection multiplicity of
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}
along
W
is defined as:

\displaystyle(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})_{W}

\displaystyle:=\len(\mathcal{O}_{W,X}/\langle h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}\rangle\mathcal{O}_{W,X})

•

If
D_{1},\ldots,D_{k}
are effective Cartier divisors on
X
and
W
is an irreducible component of
\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}\supp(D_{i})
such that
\codim(W)=k
and
W\not\subseteq\sing(X)
, then the intersection multiplicity of
D_{1},\ldots,D_{k}
along
W
is:

\displaystyle(D_{1},\ldots,D_{k})_{W}

\displaystyle:=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})_{W\cap U}

where
h_{i}
are local representatives of
D_{i}
on an open subset
U
of
X
such that
U\cap W\neq\emptyset
.

7.3
.

We continue to work in the set up of
Section

7.1
. By a
distinguished component
of (the ordered intersection of)
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
we mean an irreducible component of some
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
from
2.7
. As in
Section

4.7
we write
\mathcal{Z}^{(\rho)}
for the union of all distinguished components of codimension
\rho
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
Z
be a distinguished component of
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
such that
Z\not\subseteq\sing(X)
. Let
i^{*}
be the smallest index such that
F_{i^{*}}
is not identically zero, and
\rho:=\codim(Z)
.

(1)

If
Z
is an irreducible component of
\bigcap_{j=1}^{\rho}\supp(F_{i_{j}})
, then

\displaystyle\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})

\displaystyle=(F_{i_{1}},\ldots,F_{i_{\rho}})_{Z}

(2)

In particular,

(a)

If
\rho=1
, then

\displaystyle\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(1)}_{i^{*}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})

\displaystyle=(F_{i^{*}})_{Z}

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=(F_{i^{*}})_{Z}\deg_{1,\ldots,\hat{i^{*}},\ldots,n}(Z)

(b)

If
\rho=n
and
Z
is an isolated point in
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\supp(F_{i})
, then

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(n)}_{1,\ldots,n}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})=(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}

(3)

If
\rho=2
, then pick an open affine subset
U
of
X
such that
U\cap Z\neq\emptyset
, and each
F_{i}
is defined on
U
by some
f_{i}\in\mathbbm{k}[U]
. Since
\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}
is a UFD
10
10

10

Due to Auslander–Buchsbaum theorem.
, it contains (up to multiplication by units) a unique element
f_{i^{*},i_{2}}
which is the least common multiple of all
f\in\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}
such that

(a)

f
divides
f_{i^{*}}
,

(b)

each irreducible factor of
f
divides each
f_{i}
,
i<i_{2}
, and

(c)

no irreducible factor of
f
divides
f_{i_{2}}
.

Then

\displaystyle\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(2)}_{i^{*},i_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})

\displaystyle=(f_{i^{*},i_{2}},f_{i_{2}})_{Z}

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{2}}(f_{i^{*},i_{2}},f_{i_{2}})_{Z}\deg_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-2}}(Z)

where the sum is over all
i_{2}
such that
Z
is an irreducible component of
Z^{(\rho)}_{i^{*},i_{2}}
, and
i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-2}
are the elements of
\{1,\ldots,n\}\setminus\{i^{*},i_{2}\}
.

Proof.

It follows in a straightforward way from the definitions.
∎

7.5
.

For the next result, we pick
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}
such that
Z
is an irreducible component of
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
. In
2.11
we constructed ideals
I^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
on an affine neighborhood
U
in
X
of some point in
Z
. Recall that
V(I^{(j}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}})
has pure codimension
j
. In particular, if
i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}
are the elements of
\{1,\ldots,n\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}\}
, and
g_{i^{\prime}_{k}}
are local representatives on
U
of generic elements in
\mathcal{L}_{i^{\prime}_{k}}
, then
V(I^{(\rho-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}},g_{i^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,g_{i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}})
has pure dimension one (or is empty); to simplify the notation we write
\vec{i}:=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho})
,
\vec{g}:=(g_{i^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,g_{i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}})
and
C_{\vec{i},\vec{g}}:=V(I^{(\rho-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}},g_{i^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,g_{i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}})
.

Proposition 7.6
.

With the above notations,

\displaystyle\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})\deg_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}(Z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in Z\cap C_{\vec{i},\vec{g}}}\ord_{P}(f_{i_{\rho}}|_{C_{\vec{i},\vec{g}}})

where
\ord_{P}(\cdot)
is either zero (if
C_{\vec{i},\vec{g}}=\emptyset
), or the order of vanishing
11
11

11

If
w
is a (closed) point of a purely one dimensional scheme
W
and
f\in\mathcal{O}_{w,W}
, then
\ord_{w}(f)
is the length of
\mathcal{O}_{w,W}/f\mathcal{O}_{w,W}
as a module over
\mathcal{O}_{w,W}

[
Ful98
, Section 1.2]
.
of
f_{i_{\rho}}|_{C_{\vec{i},\vec{g}}}
at
P
. Consequently,

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\sum_{P\in Z\cap C_{\vec{i},\vec{g}}}\ord_{P}(f_{i_{\rho}}|_{C_{\vec{i},\vec{g}}})

where the sum is over all
i_{1}=i^{*}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{\rho}
such that
Z
is an irreducible component of
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
.

Proof.

This follows from identity (
2
) on page
2
and
2.11
.
∎

Example 7.7
.

Let
F_{i}=\sum_{j}m_{i,j}C_{j}+\sum_{k}m^{\prime}_{i,k}C^{\prime}_{i,k}
,
i=1,2
, be curves (i.e. purely one dimensional schemes) on a surface
X
, where
m_{i,j},m^{\prime}_{i,k}
are positive integers, and
C_{j},C^{\prime}_{i,k}
are pairwise distinct irreducible curves; in particular,
C_{j}
are precisely the irreducible components common to both
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
. Then

(5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigcap_{i}\supp(F_{i})&=\bigcup_{j}C_{j}\cup\bigcup_{k_{1},k_{2}}(C^{\prime}_{1,k_{1}}\cap C^{\prime}_{2,k_{2}})\\
\mathcal{Z}^{(1)}&=\bigcup_{j}C_{j}\\
\mathcal{Z}^{(2)}&=\bigcup_{k}(C^{\prime}_{1,k}\cap\supp(F_{2}))\end{split}

For each
j
,

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2})_{C_{j}}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=m_{1,j}(C_{j},F_{2})=m_{1,j}\deg_{2}(C_{j})

If
C_{j}\not\subseteq\sing(X)
, then
F_{1}
can be represented by some
f_{1}
near a generic (nonsingular) point of
C_{j}
, and
m_{1,j}=(f_{1})_{C_{j}}
is the order of vanishing of
f_{1}
along
C_{j}
, so that

(6)

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2})_{C_{j}}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=(f_{1})_{C_{j}}\deg_{2}(C_{j})

Similarly, if
P\in\mathcal{Z}^{(2)}
is a nonsingular point of
X
, then

(7)

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2})_{P}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=(f^{\prime}_{1},f_{2})_{P}

where
f^{\prime}_{1},f_{2}
are local equations of respectively
\sum_{k}m^{\prime}_{1,k}C^{\prime}_{1,k}
and
F_{2}
near
P
.

Example 7.8
.

More generally, assume
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n-1}
intersect properly, and the scheme theoretic intersection of
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n-1}
is
\sum_{j}m_{j}C_{j}+\sum_{k}m^{\prime}_{k}C^{\prime}_{k}
, where the
C_{j},C^{\prime}_{k}
are irreducible (reduced) curves such that
\supp(F_{n})
contains each
C_{j}
, and does
not
contain any
C^{\prime}_{k}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{(\rho)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\text{if}\ \rho<n-1,\\
\bigcup_{j}C_{j}&\text{if}\ \rho=n-1,\\
\bigcup_{k}(C^{\prime}_{k}\cap\supp(F_{n}))&\text{if}\ \rho=n\end{cases}

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}m_{j}\deg_{n}(C_{j})&\text{if}\ Z=C_{j},\\
m^{\prime}_{k}\ord_{P}(F_{n}|_{C^{\prime}_{k}})&\text{if}\ Z=P\in C^{\prime}_{k},\\
0&\text{otherwise}\\
\end{cases}

Example 7.9
.

Let
X
be a variety of pure dimension
3
, and
F_{i}=\sum_{j}m_{i,j}H_{j}+F^{\prime}_{i}
, where
m_{i,j}
are positive integers,
H_{j}
are irreducible hypersurfaces which are not contained in
\bigcup_{i}\supp(F^{\prime}_{i})
, and the
F^{\prime}_{i}
are hypersurfaces with no irreducible components common to all
F^{\prime}_{i}
. We will describe the distinguished components of the ordered intersection of the
F_{i}
and the corresponding ordered intersection multiplicity.

7.9.1
.

Since
H_{j}
are codimension one irreducible components of each
F_{i}
, it follows that

(8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{j}\supp(H_{j})

Since

(9)

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})

\displaystyle=(\sum_{j}m_{1,j}H_{j}+F^{\prime}_{1},F_{2},F_{3})=\sum_{j}m_{1,j}(H_{j},F_{2},F_{3})+(F^{\prime}_{1},F_{2},F_{3})

it follows that

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{H_{j}}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=m_{1,j}(H_{j},F_{2},F_{3})=m_{1,j}\deg_{2,3}(H_{j})

If
H_{j}\not\subseteq\sing(X)
, then

(10)

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{H_{j}}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=(f_{1})_{H_{j}}\deg_{2,3}(H_{j})

where
f_{1}
is a local equation of
F_{1}
near a generic (nonsingular) point of
H_{j}
.

7.9.2
.

Now we consider
(F^{\prime}_{1},F_{2},F_{3})
, the second summand of the right hand side of (
9
). Write
F^{\prime}_{1}=F^{\prime}_{1,2}+F^{\prime\prime}_{1}
, where
F^{\prime}_{1,2}
is the sum of the irreducible components of
F^{\prime}_{1}
(with appropriate multiplicity) which are common to
F_{2}
, and
F^{\prime\prime}_{1}
does not have any irreducible component in common with
F^{\prime}_{2}
. It follows that

\displaystyle(F^{\prime}_{1},F_{2},F_{3})

\displaystyle=(F^{\prime}_{1,2}+F^{\prime\prime}_{1},F_{2},F_{3})=(F^{\prime}_{1,2},F_{3},F_{2})+(F^{\prime\prime}_{1},F_{2},F_{3})

where the intersection of the first two components in each triplet has codimension two. Write the scheme theoretic intersections

\displaystyle F^{\prime}_{1,2}\cap F_{3}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}m^{\prime}_{k}C^{\prime}_{k},

\displaystyle F^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cap F_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}m^{\prime\prime}_{1,i}C^{\prime\prime}_{1,i}+\sum_{j}m^{\prime\prime}_{2,j}C^{\prime\prime}_{2,j}

where
C^{\prime\prime}_{1,i}
are irreducible curves which are contained in
\supp(F_{3})
, and
C^{\prime\prime}_{2,j}
are irreducible curves which are not contained in
\supp(F_{3})
. Then

(11)

\displaystyle(F^{\prime}_{1},F_{2},F_{3})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}m^{\prime}_{k}(C^{\prime}_{k},F_{2})+\sum_{i}m^{\prime\prime}_{1,i}(C^{\prime\prime}_{1,i},F_{3})+\sum_{j}m^{\prime\prime}_{2,j}(C^{\prime\prime}_{2,j},F_{3})

It follows that

(12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{k}C^{\prime}_{k}\cup\bigcup_{i}C^{\prime\prime}_{1,i}

and if
C
is one of the
C^{\prime}_{k}
or
C^{\prime\prime}_{1,i}
, then

(13)

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{C}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=m^{\prime}_{k}\deg_{2}(C)+m^{\prime\prime}_{1,i}\deg_{3}(C)

where
k
and
i
are such that
C^{\prime}_{k}=C
and
C^{\prime\prime}_{1,i}=C
(if there is no such
k
or
i
, then we accordingly set
m^{\prime}_{k}=0
or
m^{\prime}_{1,i}=0
). If
C\not\subseteq\sing(X)
, then this multiplicity can also be expressed as:

(14)

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{C}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=(F^{\prime}_{1,2},F_{3})_{C}\deg_{2}(C)+(F^{\prime\prime}_{1},F_{2})_{C}\deg_{3}(C)

which is in line with assertion (
3
) of
7.4
.

7.9.3
.

Finally, from the last sum in the right hand side of (
11
) we deduce

(15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{j}C^{\prime\prime}_{2,j}\cap\supp(F_{3})

and if
P\in\mathcal{Z}^{(3)}
, then

(16)

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{P}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=\sum_{C^{\prime\prime}_{2,j}\ni P}m^{\prime\prime}_{2,j}\ord_{P}(f_{3,P}|_{C^{\prime\prime}_{2,j}})

where
f_{3,P}
is a local equation of
F_{3}
near
P
. If
C^{\prime\prime}_{2}:=\sum_{C^{\prime\prime}_{2,j}\ni P}m^{\prime\prime}_{2,j}C^{\prime\prime}_{2,j}
is a local complete intersection near
P
defined by say
f^{\prime\prime}_{1},f^{\prime\prime}_{2}
, then of course
(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{P}^{\ord}
would equal the intersection multiplicity of
f^{\prime\prime}_{1},f^{\prime\prime}_{2},f_{3,P}
at
P
; however,
C^{\prime\prime}_{2}
may
not
be a complete intersection at
P
even when
X
is nonsingular at
P
. It follows that unlike codimension
\leq 2
cases, at a codimension three distinguished component, even if it is not contained in
\sing(X)
, the ordered intersection multiplicity can
not
in general be represented as a (sum of) product(s) of degree times a usual intersection multiplicity.

Example 7.10
.

As an example of the scenario described at the end of the preceding example, consider

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle=x^{3}-yz

\displaystyle f_{2}

\displaystyle=y^{2}-xz

\displaystyle f_{3}

\displaystyle=x^{2}+xz^{2}+y^{3}+yz^{3}

on
\mathbbm{k}^{3}
, and let
F_{i}
be the closure of
V(f_{i})
on
\mathbb{P}^{3}
. Scheme theoretically,

(17)

\displaystyle F_{1}\cap F_{2}

\displaystyle=C+Z

where
Z:=V(x,y)
is the “
z
-axis”, and
C
is the (closure of the) monomial curve
\{(t^{3},t^{4},t^{5}):t\in\mathbbm{k}\}
. It follows that

\displaystyle\bigcap_{i=1}^{3}\supp(F_{i})=Z\cup S

for a set
S
of isolated points. We will compute the number
(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{\mathbb{P}^{3}}^{iso}
(counted with appropriate multiplicities) of isolated points in
\bigcap_{i}\supp(F_{i})
. First note that
\mathcal{Z}^{(2)}=\emptyset
,
\mathcal{Z}^{(1)}=Z
, and consequently, it follows from identity (
1
) on page
1
that

(18)

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{\mathbb{P}^{3}}^{iso}

\displaystyle=(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})-(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{Z}^{\ord}-\sum_{P\in Z}(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{P}^{\ord}

Identities (
14
) and (
17
) imply that

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=(F_{1},F_{2})_{Z}\deg_{3}(Z)=1\times 4=4

It follows from (
15
) that the codimension three distinguished components of the ordered intersection of the
F_{i}
are simply the points in
C\cap\supp(F_{3})
. Consequently, the only nonzero contributions to the last sum of the right hand side of (
18
) come from the points in
Z\cap C
. There are two such points in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
, namely the origin
P_{0}
in
\mathbbm{k}^{3}
, and the point
P_{\infty}
at infinity on the
z
-axis. Now, it is well known that
C
is
not
a complete intersection near
P_{0}
(see e.g.
[
Har77
, Exercise 1.11]
), so that
(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{P_{0}}^{\ord}
, as expressed in identity (
16
), can
not
be readily expressed as the usual intersection multiplicity of polynomials at
P_{0}
(in
8.3.5
below we provide an estimate which is sometimes useful in these scenarios). In any event, for the present example, we can compute via the explicit parametrization
\phi:t\mapsto(t^{3},t^{4},t^{5})
of
C
near
P_{0}
that

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{P_{0}}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=(F_{1},F_{2})_{C}\ord_{t}(\phi^{*}(f_{3}))=1\times 6=6

Similarly, one can compute that

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{P_{\infty}}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=1

It then follows from Bézout’s theorem and (
18
) that

\displaystyle(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{\mathbb{P}^{3}}^{iso}

\displaystyle=3\times 2\times 4-4-6-1=13

8.
Computation in terms of Newton diagrams

We continue to work in the set up of
Section

7.1
.
7.4
shows that the “local factors” of ordered intersection multiplicity at a subvariety
Z
of
X
can sometimes be represented in terms of intersection multiplicities of irreducible components of complete intersection. In this section we use the results of
[
Mon21
]
to estimate this intersection multiplicity case that
Z
contains nonsingular points of
X
in terms of related “Newton diagrams”, and give a few examples of how this can be applied to the affine Bézout problem of counting isolated points of intersections.

8.1
.
(Usual) intersection multiplicity at a nonsingular point

In this section we recall basic results
[
Mon21
, Theorems IX.1, IX.8]
on the computation of intersection multiplicity of
n
hypersurfaces at a nonsingular point on an
n
-dimensional variety and some relevant notation that we use in subsequent sections.

8.1.1
.

The
n
-dimensional
mixed volume
is the unique symmetric multiadditive functional
\mv
on
n
-tuples of polytopes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\mv(\mathcal{P},\ldots,\mathcal{P})=n!\vol_{n}(\mathcal{P})
for each polytope
\mathcal{P}
, where
\vol_{n}
is the
n
-dimensional Euclidean volume.

8.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a (possibly infinite) subset of
(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{n}
. The convex hull of
\mathcal{A}+(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{n}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a convex polyhedron; the
Newton diagram

\nd(\mathcal{A})
of
\mathcal{A}
is the union of the compact faces of this polyhedron. Given variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
, the
support
of a power series
f=\sum_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}
in
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, denoted
\supp(f)
, is the set of all
\alpha
such that
c_{\alpha}\neq 0
. The Newton diagram of
f
, denoted
\nd(f)
, is the Newton diagram of
\supp(f)
.

8.1.3
.

We write
[n]:=\{1,\ldots,n\}
. If
I\subseteq[n]
and
k
is a field, we write
k^{I}
for the “
|I|
-dimensional coordinate subspace”
\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in k^{n}:x_{i}=0\ \text{if}\ i\not\in I\}
of
k^{n}
. By
\pi_{I}:k^{n}\to k^{I}
we denote the natural projection in the coordinates indexed by
I
.

8.1.4
.

Let
\nu
be a weighted order on
\mathbbm{k}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
corresponding to weights
\nu_{j}
for
x_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
. We identify
\nu
with the element in
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{*}
with coordinates
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n})
with respect to the dual basis. For
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we write
\In_{\nu}(\mathcal{S}):=\{\alpha\in\mathcal{S}:\langle\nu,\alpha\rangle=\inf_{\mathcal{S}}(\nu)\}
. The “initial form” of
f=\sum_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}
with respect to
\nu
is
\In_{\nu}(f):=\sum_{\alpha\in\In_{\nu}(\supp(f))}c_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}
. More generally, if
\supp(f)\subseteq\mathcal{S}
, we write

\displaystyle\In_{\mathcal{S},\nu}(f)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\alpha\in\In_{\nu}(\mathcal{S})}c_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}=\begin{cases}\In_{\nu}(f)&\text{if}\ \supp(f)\cap\In_{\nu}(\mathcal{S})\neq\emptyset,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We say that
\nu
is
centered at the origin
if each
\nu_{i}
is positive and that
\nu
is
primitive
if it is nonzero and the greatest common divisor of
\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n}
is
1
. If
\nu
is centered at the origin, then it also extends to a weighted order on the ring of power series in
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
.

8.1.5
.

Given Newton diagrams
\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, define

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n})_{0}

\displaystyle:=\min\{(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})_{0}:\supp(f_{j})\subseteq\Gamma_{j}+(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{n},\ j=1,\ldots,n\}

We now recall the basic result regarding
(\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n})_{0}
. It gives an expression of
(\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n})_{0}
in terms of the following quantity:

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n})_{0}^{*}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{0}}\min_{\Gamma_{1}}(\nu)~\mv^{\prime}_{\nu}(\In_{\nu}(\Gamma_{2}),\ldots,\In_{\nu}(\Gamma_{n}))

where
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{0}
is the set of primitive weighted orders centered at the origin, and
\mv^{\prime}_{\nu}
is the
(n-1)
-dimensional mixed volume of
\psi_{\nu}(\In_{\nu}(\Gamma_{j})+\alpha_{j})
,
j=2,\ldots,n
, where
\alpha_{j}
is an arbitrary element in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
such that
\In_{\nu}(\Gamma_{j})+\alpha_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\nu^{\perp}}:=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\langle\nu,\alpha\rangle=0\}
, and
\psi_{\nu}:\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\nu^{\perp}}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
is an arbitrary isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-modules.

Theorem 8.1.6

(
[
Mon21
, Theorems IX.1, IX.8]
)
.

Let
\Gamma:=(\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n})
be a collection of Newton diagrams in
(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{n}
. For each
I\subseteq[n]
, let
T^{I}_{\Gamma}:=\{j:\Gamma_{j}\cap\mathbb{R}^{I}\neq\emptyset\}
be the set of all indices
j
such that
\Gamma_{j}
touches
\mathbb{R}^{I}
. Define

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{\Gamma,1}:=\{I\subseteq[n]:I\neq\emptyset,\ |T^{I}_{\Gamma}|=|I|,\ 1\in T^{I}_{\Gamma}\}

Then

(1)

If
0\not\in\bigcup_{j}\Gamma_{j}
and there is
I\subset[n]
such that
|T^{I}_{\Gamma}|<|I|
, then
(\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n})_{0}=\infty
.

(2)

Otherwise

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n})_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathscr{T}_{\Gamma,1}}(\Gamma_{1}\cap\mathbb{R}^{I},\Gamma_{j_{2}}\cap\mathbb{R}^{I},\ldots,\Gamma_{j_{|I|}}\cap\mathbb{R}^{I})_{0}^{*}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\times(\pi_{[n]\setminus I}(\Gamma_{j^{\prime}_{1}}),\ldots,\pi_{[n]\setminus I}(\Gamma_{j^{\prime}_{n-|I|}}))_{0}

where for each
I\in\mathscr{T}_{\Gamma,1}
,
j_{1}=1,j_{2},\ldots,j_{|I|}
are elements of
T^{I}_{\Gamma}
, and
j^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,j^{\prime}_{n-|I|}
are elements of
[n]\setminus T^{I}_{\Gamma}
.

Pick
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\in\mathbbm{k}[[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]]
such that
\supp(f_{j})\subseteq\Gamma_{j}+(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{n}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
. Then it is clear that
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})_{0}\geq(\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n})_{0}
.

(3)

In the situation of assertion (
2
),
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})_{0}=(\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n})_{0}
if and only if the following ‘‘non-degeneracy’’ condition holds
12
12

12

There is a more “efficient” version of the non-degeneracy condition which allows to disregard certain subsets of
[n]

[
Mon21
, Theorem IX.9]
.
: for each nonempty subset
I
of
[n]
and each weighted order
\nu
centered at the origin, there is no common root of
\In_{\Gamma_{j}\cap\mathbb{R}^{I},\nu}(f_{j}|_{\mathbbm{k}^{I}})
,
j=1,\ldots,n
, on
(\mathbbm{k}\setminus\{0\})^{n}
. ∎

Example 8.1.7
.

Consider

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle=x^{4}+xy+y^{2}(1-y+7yz)

\displaystyle f_{2}

\displaystyle=2xz^{2}+y(1-y)(4+z^{2})

\displaystyle f_{3}

\displaystyle=x^{2}(5+8xz)+y(1-y+3z^{3})

\displaystyle=(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})-(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{Z}^{\ord}-\sum_{P\in Z}(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{P}^{\ord}

Note that

\displaystyle(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})_{\mathbbm{k}^{3}}^{iso}

\displaystyle=(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{\mathbb{P}^{3}}^{iso}-\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in\mathbb{P}^{3}\setminus\mathbbm{k}^{3}\\

\displaystyle(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})_{\mathbbm{k}^{3}}^{iso}

(19)

\displaystyle=64-(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{Z}^{\ord}-\sum_{P\in Z}(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{P}^{\ord}-(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{P_{y}}

since

due to Bézout’s theorem. We now compute

To compute

8.2
.
(possibly non-isolated) complete intersections

Consider an
(n-k)
-dimensional irreducible component
Z
of
V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})
, where the
f_{j}
are regular functions on an
n
-dimensional affine variety
X
. Assume in addition that
Z\not\subseteq\sing(X)
. Under this condition we show that the intersection multiplicity
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})_{Z}
of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}
along
Z
can be estimated in terms of certain Newton diagrams. Indeed, since
Z\not\subseteq\sing(X)
, there are ‘‘regular coordinates’’
13
13

13

We say
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})
are
regular coordinates
on a nonsingular variety
Y
if
\dim(Y)=n
and for each
y\in Y
,
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{y,Y}\cong\mathbbm{k}[[g_{1}-g_{1}(y),\ldots,g_{n}-g_{n}(y)]]
.

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
on an open subset
U
of
X
such that
U\cap Z=V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\cap U\neq\emptyset
. Then there are
f_{j,\alpha}\in\mathbbm{k}[U]
such that for each
a\in U
,

\displaystyle f_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{n}}f_{j,\alpha}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-a_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}\in\mathbbm{k}[[x_{1}-a_{1},\ldots,x_{n}-a_{n}]]

where
a_{i}:=x_{i}(a)
(see e.g.
[
BM97
, Lemma 3.5]
). Define

\displaystyle f^{[k]}_{j}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{[k]}}f_{j,\alpha}\prod_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}\in\mathbbm{k}[U][[x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}]]

(recall from
Section

8.1.3
that
\mathbb{Z}^{[k]}
is the subset of all elements in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
whose last
(n-k)
-coordinates are identically zero). The
Newton diagram

\nd(f^{[k]}_{j})
with respect to
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
is the Newton diagram of the set of all
\alpha
such that
f_{j,\alpha}\not\equiv 0
.

Proposition 8.2.1
.

Let
\pi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be the projection on the first
k
coordinates. Then

(1)

(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})_{Z}\geq(\pi(\nd(f^{[k]}_{1})),\ldots,\pi(\nd(f^{[k]}_{k})))_{0}

(2)

If the right hand side is finite, then the above relation holds with an equality if and only if there is
a\in U\cap Z
such that the following pair of “non-degeneracy” conditions hold:

(a)

for each
j=1,\ldots,k
, and each vertex
\alpha
of
\pi(\nd(f^{[k]}_{j}))
, the coefficient
f_{j,\alpha}
is nonzero at
a
, and

(b)

for each nonempty subset
I
of
[k]
and each weighted order
\nu
centered at the origin, there is no common root of
\In_{\nu}(f^{[k]}_{j}|_{\mathbbm{k}^{I}})
,
j=1,\ldots,k
, on
\{a\}\times(\mathbbm{k}\setminus\{0\})^{k}
.

(3)

Moreover, the set of all
a\in U\cap Z
such that the above non-degeneracy conditions hold is a (possibly empty) Zariski open subset of
Z
, and every such
a
(if exists) has an open neighborhood
U_{a}
on
X
such that
V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})\cap U_{a}
is a complete intersection with only one irreducible component, namely
Z\cap U_{a}
.

Proof.

Since
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})_{Z}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k},x_{k+1}-a_{k+1},\ldots,x_{n}-a_{n})_{0}
for generic
a\in U\cap Z
, all statements except for the last one are immediate corollaries of
8.1.6
. The Zariski openness of the set of
a\in U\cap Z
satisfying the non-degeneracy conditions is a consequence of the Zariski openness of the
n
-tuples of polynomials which are non-degenerate at the origin
[
Mon21
, Theorem IX.8]
. For the local complete intersection property, pick
a\in U\cap Z
which satisfies condition
(a)
and in addition, also belongs to another irreducible component
Z^{\prime}
of
V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})
. Pick an irreducible curve
C
on
Z^{\prime}
such that
a\in C
and
C\not\subseteq Z
. Let
I
be the subset of
[k]
consisting of all
i
such that
x_{i}|_{C}\not\equiv 0
. If
B
is a branch of
C
at
a
, then condition
(b)
is violated with
I
and
\nu:=(\ord_{B}(x_{i}))_{i\in I}
. This completes the proof of the last assertion of
8.2.1
.
∎

Example 8.2.2
.

We continue with the computation in characteristic zero started in
8.1.7
. Now we compute the ordered intersection multiplicity of
F_{1},F_{2},F_{3}
along
Z
, i.e. the term
(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{Z}^{\ord}
from the right hand side of identity (
19
). Since

is an irreducible component of
V(f_{1},f_{2})
, assertion
1
of
7.4
implies that

\displaystyle\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{1,2}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})

\displaystyle=(f_{1},f_{2})_{Z}

\pi(\nd(f_{1}))
\pi(\nd(f_{2}))

Figure 2.
“Generic” Newton diagrams of
f_{1},f_{2}
with respect to
(x,y)
-coordinates

Since
Z=V(x,y)
, in the notation of

we need to consider
f_{j}^{\{1,2\}}
,

, which are simply
f_{j}
but where we consider monomials in

as “coefficients”.
Figure

displays the Newton diagrams of

\pi(\nd(f_{j}))
where

(x,y)
-coordinates. Since

has only one edge

and

imply that

(21)

\displaystyle(f_{1},f_{2})_{Z}

\displaystyle\geq(\pi(\nd(f_{1})),\pi(\nd(f_{2})))_{0}=\nu(f_{1})\times\len(E)=2\times 1=2

The initial forms of
f^{\{1,2\}}_{j}
corresponding to the weighted order

are:

\displaystyle\In_{\nu}(f^{\{1,2\}}_{1})

\displaystyle=xy+y^{2}\qquad\quad

\displaystyle\In_{\nu}(f^{\{1,2\}}_{2})

\displaystyle=2xz^{2}+y(4+z^{2})

The second assertion of

implies that the inequality (

) is satisfied with an equality if and only if there is

such that following non-degeneracy condition is satisfied:

(22)

there is no common solution to
\In_{\nu}(f^{\{1,2\}}_{1})=\In_{\nu}(f^{\{1,2\}}_{2})=0
with

.

It is clear that the above condition is satisfied for almost all

. It follows that

(23)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(f_{1},f_{2})_{Z}&=2,\ \text{and therefore,}\\
(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{Z}^{\ord}&=(f_{1},f_{2})_{Z}\times\deg_{3}(Z)=2\times 4=8\end{split}

In order to compute
(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})_{\mathbbm{k}^{3}}^{iso}
using (
19
), it remains to compute
\sum_{P\in Z}(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{P}^{\ord}
. Write
F_{1}\cap F_{2}=mZ+C
, where the
C
is a (possibly non-reduced) curve whose support does not contain

. Then

(24)

\displaystyle\sum_{P\in Z}(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})_{P}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in Z}\ord_{P}(f_{3,P}|_{C})

where
f_{3,P}
are local equations of
F_{3}
near

(see, e.g.

). Note that the summand on the right hand side is nonzero only for the (finitely many) points

in

. The third assertion of

implies that every such

(i)

) does

,

or

,

, but there is a common solution to

at

with

(this is possible if and only if

);

(ii)

or

does
not
belong to the coordinate chart of

with coordinates
(x,y,z)
, i.e.
P
is the point

with respect to homogeneous coordinates
[x:y:z:w]
.

We will compute
\ord_{P}(F_{3}|_{C})
in each of these cases in
8.3.7
below.

8.3
.
(a special type of) non set-theoretic complete intersections

Consider
8.2.2
above, and take one of the points, say
P=(0,0,0)
, that satisfies one of the two conditions given at the end of the example. We need to compute
\ord_{P}(f_{3}|_{C})
; at the least we would like to estimate it
from below
. If
C
were a complete intersection of
f_{1},f_{2}
near
P
, then
\ord_{P}(f_{3}|_{C})
would simply be the intersection multiplicity
(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})_{P}
of
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}
at
P
, and we could have used
8.1.6
to estimate it. However,
C
is
not
a complete intersection of
f_{1},f_{2}
near
P
. As we have seen,
V(f_{1},f_{2})
has another irreducible component
Z
containing
P
, so that
\ord_{P}(f_{3}|_{C})<(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})_{P}
, i.e.
(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})_{P}
can
not
be used to estimate
\ord_{P}(f_{3}|_{C})
from below (in fact in this particular case
f_{3}|_{Z}\equiv 0
, so that
(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})_{P}=\infty
). The main result of this section (
8.3.5
) can sometimes be used to give such an estimate for
\ord_{P}(f_{3}|_{C})
in the case that the “punctured” germ of
C
at
P
is a complete intersection in the complement of a union of “coordinate subspaces”.

8.3.1
.

First we state a variant of the non-degeneracy condition from
8.1.6
: we say that
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\in\mathbbm{k}[[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]]
are
(*,k)
-non-degenerate at the origin if they satisfy the following condition:

(
\nd^{*,k}_{0}
)

for each weighted order
\nu
centered at the origin such that
\dim(\In_{\nu}(\sum\supp(f_{j})))\leq k
, there is no common root of
\In_{\nu}(f_{j})
,
j=1,\ldots,m
, on
(\mathbbm{k}\setminus\{0\})^{n}
.

We say that
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
are
*
-non-degenerate at the origin if they are
(*,k)
-non-degenerate at the origin for each
k\geq 0
.

8.3.2
.

Let
z
be a nonsingular point on a variety
X
of dimension
n
, and
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
be a system of regular coordinates near
z
such that each
x_{i}
vanishes at
z
, i.e.
z
is the “origin” with respect to
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. Given a subset
I
of
[n]
, we write
K^{I}\subseteq X
for the set of zeroes of all
x_{i}
such that
i\not\in I
(i.e.
K^{I}
is the analogue of the “coordinate subspace”
\mathbbm{k}^{I}
); in particular,

\displaystyle K^{I}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}X&\text{if}\ I=[n],\\
V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})&\text{if}\ I=\emptyset.\end{cases}

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a given collection of subsets of
[n]
. Consider
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n-1}\in\mathcal{O}_{z,X}
. We are interested in the irreducible components of
V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n-1})
which contain
z
, and in addition, are
not
contained in
K^{I}
for any
I\in\mathcal{I}
; let
V^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I}}
be the union of all such components. Write
\bar{}\mathcal{I}:=\{I\subseteq[n]:I\subseteq I^{\prime}
for some
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}\}
, i.e.
\bar{}\mathcal{I}
is the “closure” of
\mathcal{I}
under the operation of taking subsets; note that
\bar{}\mathcal{I}
is the largest collection of subsets of
[n]
such that

\displaystyle\bigcup_{I\in\bar{}\mathcal{I}}K^{I}=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}K^{I}

Proposition 8.3.3
.

For each
I\subseteq[n]
, let
T^{I}:=\{j:f_{j}|_{K^{I}}\not\equiv 0
near
z\}
(i.e.
T^{I}
is the analogue of
T^{I}_{\Gamma}
from
8.1.6
). With the set up from the preceding paragraph, assume the following conditions hold for all
I\not\in\bar{}\mathcal{I}
:

(1)

|T^{I}|\geq|I|-1
,

(2)

f_{1}|_{K^{I}},\ldots,f_{n-1}|_{K^{I}}
are
(*,|T^{I}|-1)
-non-degenerate at the origin.

where we treat the
f_{i}
as power series in
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. Then either
V^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I}}=\emptyset
, or
V^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I}}
is a curve (i.e.
V^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I}}
is purely one dimensional).

Proof.

Assume
V^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I}}\neq\emptyset
. Pick an irreducible component
V^{\prime}
of
V^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I}}
. First note that
\dim(V^{\prime})\geq 1
. Let
I
be the smallest subset of
[n]
such that
V^{\prime}\subseteq K^{I}
. Then
I\not\in\bar{}\mathcal{I}
. Without loss of generality we may assume that
I=\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Consider a subset
\mathcal{A}
of
(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{m}
such that

(i)

the radical of the ideal
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{A}}
of
\mathcal{O}_{z,K^{I}}
generated by monomials
x^{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
, is the maximal ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{z,K^{I}}
, and

(ii)

every
m-1
dimensional face of
\nd(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\supp(f_{i}|_{K^{I}}))
(if there is one) is also a face of
\nd(\mathcal{A})
.

Consider the blow up
\tilde{K}^{I}
of
K^{I}
at
z
with respect to
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{A}}
. The irreducible components of the exceptional divisor
E
on
\tilde{K}^{I}
are toric varieties
X_{\mathcal{Q}}
corresponding to
m-1
dimensional faces
\mathcal{Q}
of
\nd(\mathcal{A})
, and the proper torus orbits of each such
X_{\mathcal{Q}}
are toric varieties
X_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
corresponding to proper faces
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{Q}
. Recall that
|T^{I}|\geq m-1
(property (
1
) of the
f_{j}
). It follows from property (
2
) of the
f_{j}
and property
(ii)
of
\mathcal{A}
that

(iii)

if
|T^{I}|>m-1
, then the strict transform
\tilde{V}^{\prime}
of
V^{\prime}
on
\tilde{K}^{I}
does
not
intersect
X_{\mathcal{Q}}
for any face
\mathcal{Q}
of
\nd(\mathcal{A})
,

(iv)

if
|T^{I}|=m-1
, then
\tilde{V}^{\prime}
does
not
intersect
X_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
for any face
\mathcal{Q}
of
\nd(\mathcal{A})
such that
\dim(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})\leq m-2

(see e.g.
[
Mon21
, Corollary VI.34]
). Since case
(iii)
leads to a contradiction, it follows that
|T^{I}|=m-1
. Bernstein’s theorem then implies that the number of points in
\tilde{V}^{\prime}\cap X_{\mathcal{Q}}
(counted with appropriate multiplicity) is
\mv^{\prime}_{\nu}(\In_{\nu}(\nd(f_{j_{1}})),\ldots,\In_{\nu}(\nd(f_{j_{m-1}})))
, where
\nu
is the primitive inner normal to
\mathcal{Q}
, and
T^{I}=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{m-1}\}
. In particular, it follows that
\tilde{V}^{\prime}\cap E
is finite, and therefore,
\tilde{V}^{\prime}
is a curve, as required.
∎

8.3.4
.

Let
z
be a nonsingular point on a variety
X
of dimension
n
and
C
be a curve, i.e. purely one dimensional scheme, containing
z
. Assume we are interested in estimating
\ord_{z}(f|_{C})
for some
f\in\mathcal{O}_{z,X}
.
8.3.5
below provides an estimate under the following assumptions: there are
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},\allowbreak f_{1},\ldots,f_{n-1}\in\mathcal{O}_{z,X}
and a collection
\mathcal{I}
of subsets of
[n]
such that

(a)

each
x_{i}
vanishes at
z
, and
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
form a system of regular coordinates near
z
,

(b)

no irreducible component of
C
containing
z
is contained in
K^{I}:=V(x_{i^{\prime}}:i^{\prime}\not\in I)
for any
I\in\mathcal{I}
,

(c)

f_{1},\ldots,f_{n-1}
generate the ideal of
C
on
U\setminus\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}K^{I}
for some neighborhood
U
of
z
in
X
(or more generally, the irreducible components of
V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n-1})
which contain
z
and are not contained in
\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}K^{I}
are precisely the irreducible components of
C
containing
z
, and in addition, the multiplicity of each such component in
V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n-1})
is also equal to its multiplicity in
C
),

(d)

when expressed as power series in
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
,
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n-1}
satisfy conditions (
1
) and (
2
) from
8.3.3
for each
I\not\in\bar{}\mathcal{I}
, where
\bar{}\mathcal{I}
is the closure of
\mathcal{I}
under the operation of taking subsets (see
Section

8.3.2
).

Proposition 8.3.5
.

Let
\mathscr{T}:=\{I\subseteq[n]:I\not\in\bar{}\mathcal{I},\ I\neq\emptyset,\ |T^{I}|=|I|-1\}
, where
T^{I}
is as in
8.3.3
. Under the assumptions in
Section

8.3.4
,

(25)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ord_{z}(f|_{C})&\geq\sum_{I\in\mathscr{T}}(\nd(f)\cap\mathbb{R}^{I},\nd(f_{j_{1}})\cap\mathbb{R}^{I},\ldots,\nd(f_{j_{|I|-1}})\cap\mathbb{R}^{I})_{0}^{*}\\
&\qquad\qquad\quad\times(\pi_{[n]\setminus I}(\nd(f_{j^{\prime}_{1}})),\ldots,\pi_{[n]\setminus I}(\nd(f_{j^{\prime}_{n-|I|}})))_{0}\end{split}

where for each
I\in\mathscr{T}
,
j_{1},\ldots,j_{|I|-1}
are elements of
T^{I}
, and
j^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,j^{\prime}_{n-|I|}
are elements of
[n-1]\setminus T^{I}
, and
(\cdot,\ldots,\cdot)_{0}^{*}
is defined as in
Section

8.1.5
. Moreover, the above relation is satisfied with an equality if and only if
f_{1}|_{K^{I}},\ldots,f_{n-1}|_{K^{I}},f|_{K^{I}}
are
*
-non-degenerate at the origin for every
I\in\mathscr{T}
.

Proof.

This follows from the arguments of the proof of
8.3.3
and standard toric geometry arguments (see e.g.
[
Mon21
, Corollary VII.27]
).
∎

8.3.6
.
Remarks on the applicability of
8.3.5

(1)

The assumptions in
Section

8.3.4
in particular implies that on the set-theoretic level there is a decomposition near
z
of the form
V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n-1})=C\cup W
with
W\subseteq\bigcup_{i}V(x_{i})
. We do not know if this is possible to achieve for an arbitrary
C
, since this puts non-trivial constraints on the singularities of
W
(e.g. the local embedding dimension of each irreducible component of
W
is
\leq n-1
).

(2)

The following example shows that assumption
(d)
in
Section

8.3.4
is necessary: consider

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle=(x-y+z)(x+y-z)

\displaystyle f_{2}

\displaystyle=(x-y-2z)

\displaystyle f_{3}

\displaystyle=x^{2}+3y^{2}+5z^{2}

over a field of characteristic zero or greater than
5
. Then
V(f_{1},f_{2})
is a curve with two components:
C_{1}
with parametrization
t\mapsto(t,t,0)
, and
C_{2}
with parametrization
t\mapsto(-3t,t,-2t)
. Let
z
be the origin,
C=C_{2}
, and
f=f_{3}
. Then
\ord_{z}(f_{3}|_{C})=2
, but the bound on the right hand side of (
25
) (applied with
\mathcal{I}=\{\{1,2\}\}
) is

\displaystyle(\nd(f_{3}),\nd(f_{1}),\nd(f_{2}))_{0}^{*}

\displaystyle\qquad=\min_{\nd(f_{3})}(\nu)\times\mv^{\prime}_{\nu}(\In_{\nu}(\nd(f_{2})),\In_{\nu}(\nd(f_{3})))\quad(\text{where}\ \nu:=(1,1,1))

\displaystyle\qquad=2\times 2=4>2=\ord_{z}(f_{3}|_{C})

9.
An example: the number of tangent lines to four generic spheres

9.1
.

In this section we apply the results of
Sections

7
and
8
to compute the number of tangent lines to four generic spheres in
\mathbbm{k}^{3}
in the case that
\character(\mathbbm{k})=0
. This number was determined to be 12 by Macdonald, Pach and Theobald
[
MPT01
]
by expressing the governing equations in a cleverly chosen system of coordinates so that a direct application of Bézout’s theorem solves the problem. On the other hand, a natural setting of this counting problem is the Grassmannian of lines in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
(or equivalently, planes in
\mathbbm{k}^{4}
). Formulating the problem in corresponding Plücker coordinates gives
5
quadric equations in
\mathbb{P}^{5}
whose common zeroes include a one-dimensional component at infinity. We explain the difference of
2^{5}-12=20
between the Bézout bound and the correct number as the sum of ordered intersection multiplicities corresponding to the excess intersection. This section was inspired by a paragraph by Sottile and Theobald (in
[
ST02
]
which solved the tangent line problem in higher dimensions) which mentions a similar explanation in terms of usual excess intersection multiplicities in a manuscript by Aluffi and Fulton.

9.2
.

Consider the open subset
U
of the space of lines in
\mathbbm{k}^{3}
parameterized by

\displaystyle(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathbbm{k}^{4}\mapsto L_{u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}}:=\{(u_{1}+tv_{1},u_{2}+tv_{2},t):t\in\mathbbm{k}\}

The points of intersection of
L_{u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}}
and the sphere in
\mathbbm{k}^{3}
with center
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
and radius
r_{1}
correspond to solutions (in
t
) of the following equation:

\displaystyle(u_{1}+tv_{1}-a_{1})^{2}+(u_{2}+tv_{2}-a_{2})^{2}+(t-a_{3})^{2}=r_{1}^{2}

Consequently,
L_{u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}}
is tangent to the sphere if and only if the discriminant of the above equation is zero, i.e.

\displaystyle(v_{1}(u_{1}-a_{1})+v_{2}(u_{2}-a_{2})-a_{3})^{2}-(v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2}+1)((u_{1}-a_{1})^{2}+(u_{2}-a_{2})^{2}+a_{3}^{2}-r_{1}^{2})=0

which simplifies to

\displaystyle((u_{2}v_{1}-u_{1}v_{2})-(a_{2}v_{1}-a_{1}v_{2}))^{2}+(u_{1}+a_{3}v_{1}-a_{1})^{2}+(u_{2}+a_{3}v_{2}-a_{2})^{2}-r_{1}^{2}(v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2}+1)

\displaystyle=0

It turns out that (up to changing some signs) the Plücker embedding of the Grassmannian of lines in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
is determined by
(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2},w)
, where
w:=u_{2}v_{1}-u_{1}v_{2}
. Consequently, in the Plücker coordinates, the lines tangent to four spheres are precisely the points in
V(f_{0},\ldots,f_{4})\subseteq\mathbbm{k}^{5}
, where

\displaystyle f_{0}

\displaystyle:=w-u_{2}v_{1}+u_{1}v_{2}

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle:=(w-(a_{2}v_{1}-a_{1}v_{2}))^{2}+(u_{1}+a_{3}v_{1}-a_{1})^{2}+(u_{2}+a_{3}v_{2}-a_{2})^{2}-r_{1}^{2}(v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2}+1)

\displaystyle f_{2}

\displaystyle:=(w-(b_{2}v_{1}-b_{1}v_{2}))^{2}+(u_{1}+b_{3}v_{1}-b_{1})^{2}+(u_{2}+b_{3}v_{2}-b_{2})^{2}-r_{2}^{2}(v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2}+1)

\displaystyle f_{3}

\displaystyle:=(w-(c_{2}v_{1}-c_{1}v_{2}))^{2}+(u_{1}+c_{3}v_{1}-c_{1})^{2}+(u_{2}+c_{3}v_{2}-c_{2})^{2}-r_{3}^{2}(v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2}+1)

\displaystyle f_{4}

\displaystyle:=(w-(d_{2}v_{1}-d_{1}v_{2}))^{2}+(u_{1}+d_{3}v_{1}-d_{1})^{2}+(u_{2}+d_{3}v_{2}-d_{2})^{2}-r_{4}^{2}(v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2}+1)

where the
r_{1},\ldots,r_{4}
are the radii and
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),\ldots,(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})
are the centers of the spheres. Since
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
does not have any irreducible components in common, the codimension of
V(f_{0},f_{1})
is
2
in
\mathbbm{k}^{5}
. Then for generic choices e.g. of
b_{1},c_{1},d_{1}
, it follows that
V(f_{0},\ldots,f_{4})
only has isolated points in
\mathbbm{k}^{5}
. We will show that the number (counted with appropriate multiplicities) of these points is 12. Note that the Bézout bound for the number of solutions of the
f_{i}
in
\mathbb{P}^{5}
is
2^{5}=32
.

9.3
.

Choosing appropriate coordinates on
\mathbbm{k}^{3}
, without loss of generality we may assume
a_{1}=a_{2}=a_{3}=b_{1}=b_{2}=0
and
r_{1}=1
. It follows that

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle=w^{2}+u_{1}^{2}+u_{2}^{2}-(v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2}+1)

\displaystyle f_{2}

\displaystyle=w^{2}+(u_{1}+b_{3}v_{1})^{2}+(u_{2}+b_{3}v_{2})^{2}-r_{2}^{2}(v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2}+1)

9.4
.

Let
F_{i}
be the divisor on
\mathbb{P}^{5}
corresponding to
f_{i}
. To determine the intersection “at infinity” on
\mathbb{P}^{5}
of the
F_{i}
, we consider their leading forms:

\displaystyle\ld(f_{0})

\displaystyle=u_{2}v_{1}-u_{1}v_{2}

\displaystyle\ld(f_{1})

\displaystyle=w^{2}+u_{1}^{2}+u_{2}^{2}-v_{1}^{2}-v_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\ld(f_{2})

\displaystyle=w^{2}+(u_{1}+b_{3}v_{1})^{2}+(u_{2}+b_{3}v_{2})^{2}-r_{2}^{2}(v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle\ld(f_{3})

\displaystyle=(w-(c_{2}v_{1}-c_{1}v_{2}))^{2}+(u_{1}+c_{3}v_{1})^{2}+(u_{2}+c_{3}v_{2})^{2}-r_{3}^{2}(v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle\ld(f_{4})

\displaystyle=(w-(d_{2}v_{1}-d_{1}v_{2}))^{2}+(u_{1}+d_{3}v_{1})^{2}+(u_{2}+d_{3}v_{2})^{2}-r_{4}^{2}(v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2})

V(\ld(f_{0}),\ld(f_{1}))
has codimension two in the hyperplane at infinity on
\mathbb{P}^{5}
. For generic choices of the
b_{3},c_{i},d_{j}
it follows that the common zero set of the
\ld(f_{i})
is empty if either
v_{1}
or
v_{2}
is nonzero, and consequently,

\displaystyle\bigcap_{i}\supp(F_{i})\cap(\mathbb{P}^{5}\setminus\mathbbm{k}^{5})=Z:=V(z,v_{1},v_{2},w^{2}+u_{1}^{2}+u_{2}^{2})

where
z
is the “homogenizing variable” which forms a system of homogeneous coordinates
[u_{1}:u_{2}:v_{1}:v_{2}:w:z]
on
\mathbb{P}^{5}
; note that
Z
is a one dimensional “imaginary circle at infinity”. It then follows from identity (
1
) on page
1
and Bézout’s theorem that

\displaystyle(f_{0},\ldots,f_{4})_{\mathbbm{k}^{5}}^{iso}

\displaystyle=(F_{0},\ldots,F_{4})-(F_{0},\ldots,F_{4})_{Z}^{\ord}-\sum_{P\in Z}(F_{0},\ldots,F_{4})_{P}^{\ord}

(30)

\displaystyle=32-(F_{0},\ldots,F_{4})_{Z}^{\ord}-\sum_{P\in Z}(F_{0},\ldots,F_{4})_{P}^{\ord}

9.5
.

Since
Z
is an irreducible component of
\bigcap_{j=0}^{3}\supp(F_{i})
, assertion (
1
) of
7.4
implies that

\displaystyle(F_{0},\ldots,F_{4})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=(F_{0},\ldots,F_{3})_{Z}\deg_{4}(Z)=(F_{0},\ldots,F_{3})_{Z}\deg(Z)\deg(F_{4})

(31)

\displaystyle=4(F_{0},\ldots,F_{3})_{Z}

To compute
(F_{0},\ldots,F_{3})_{Z}
, we work on the coordinate chart
U_{w}:=\mathbb{P}^{5}\setminus V(w)
. With the coordinates
(\tilde{u}_{1},\tilde{u}_{2},\tilde{v}_{1},\tilde{v}_{2},\tilde{z}):=(u_{1}/w,u_{2}/w,v_{1}/w,v_{2}/w,1/w)
on
U_{w}
, the polynomials defining
F_{i}\cap U_{w}
are:

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{0}

\displaystyle:=\tilde{z}-\tilde{u}_{2}\tilde{v}_{1}+\tilde{u}_{1}\tilde{v}_{2}

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{1}

\displaystyle:=1+\tilde{u}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{u}_{2}^{2}-(\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{v}_{2}^{2}+\tilde{z}^{2})

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{2}

\displaystyle:=1+(\tilde{u}_{1}+b_{3}\tilde{v}_{1})^{2}+(\tilde{u}_{2}+b_{3}\tilde{v}_{2})^{2}-r_{2}^{2}(\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{v}_{2}^{2}+\tilde{z}^{2})

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{3}

\displaystyle:=(1-(c_{2}\tilde{v}_{1}-c_{1}\tilde{v}_{2}))^{2}+(\tilde{u}_{1}+c_{3}\tilde{v}_{1}-c_{1}\tilde{z})^{2}+(\tilde{u}_{2}+c_{3}\tilde{v}_{2}-c_{2}\tilde{z})^{2}-r_{3}^{2}(\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{v}_{2}^{2}+\tilde{z}^{2})

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{4}

\displaystyle:=(1-(d_{2}\tilde{v}_{1}-d_{1}\tilde{v}_{2}))^{2}+(\tilde{u}_{1}+d_{3}\tilde{v}_{1}-d_{1}\tilde{z})^{2}+(\tilde{u}_{2}+d_{3}\tilde{v}_{2}-d_{2}\tilde{z})^{2}-r_{4}^{2}(\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{v}_{2}^{2}+\tilde{z}^{2})

Note that
\tilde{S}:=V(\tilde{f}_{0},\tilde{f}_{1})
is a nonsingular threefold near each point of
Z\cap U_{w}
, so that
(F_{0},\ldots,F_{3})_{Z}
is the intersection multiplicity along
Z
of the (restrictions of the) divisors of
\tilde{f}_{2}
and
\tilde{f}_{3}
on
\tilde{S}
; in other words,

(32)

\displaystyle(F_{0},\ldots,F_{3})_{Z}

\displaystyle=(\tilde{f}_{2}|_{\tilde{S}},\tilde{f}_{3}|_{\tilde{S}})_{Z}

9.5.1
.

To compute the divisor of
\tilde{f}_{2}
on
\tilde{S}
note that

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{2}

\displaystyle=1+\tilde{u}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{u}_{2}^{2}+2b_{3}(\tilde{u}_{1}\tilde{v}_{1}+\tilde{u}_{2}\tilde{v}_{2})-r_{2}^{2}(\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{v}_{2}^{2}+\tilde{z}^{2})

\displaystyle\equiv 2b_{3}(\tilde{u}_{1}\tilde{v}_{1}+\tilde{u}_{2}\tilde{v}_{2})+(1-r_{2}^{2})(\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{v}_{2}^{2}+\tilde{z}^{2})\mod\tilde{f}_{1}

Now

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{v}_{2}^{2}+\tilde{z}^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{v}_{2}^{2}+(\tilde{u}_{2}\tilde{v}_{1}-\tilde{u}_{1}\tilde{v}_{2})^{2}\mod\tilde{f}_{0}

\displaystyle=\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}(1+\tilde{u}_{2}^{2})+\tilde{v}_{2}^{2}(1+\tilde{u}_{1}^{2})-2\tilde{u}_{1}\tilde{u}_{2}\tilde{v}_{1}\tilde{v}_{2}

\displaystyle=\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}(1+\tilde{u}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{u}_{2}^{2})+\tilde{v}_{2}^{2}(1+\tilde{u}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{u}_{2}^{2})-(\tilde{u}_{1}^{2}\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{u}_{2}^{2}\tilde{v}_{2}^{2}+2\tilde{u}_{1}\tilde{u}_{2}\tilde{v}_{1}\tilde{v}_{2})

\displaystyle=(1+\tilde{u}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{u}_{2}^{2})(\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{v}_{2}^{2})-(\tilde{u}_{1}\tilde{v}_{1}+\tilde{u}_{2}\tilde{v}_{2})^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv(v_{1}^{2}+\tilde{v}_{2}^{2}+\tilde{z}^{2})(\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{v}_{2}^{2})-(\tilde{u}_{1}\tilde{v}_{1}+\tilde{u}_{2}\tilde{v}_{2})^{2}\mod\tilde{f}_{1}

so that, modulo
\tilde{f}_{0}
and
\tilde{f}_{1}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{v}_{2}^{2}+\tilde{z}^{2}\equiv-\frac{(\tilde{u}_{1}\tilde{v}_{1}+\tilde{u}_{2}\tilde{v}_{2})^{2}}{1-\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}-\tilde{v}_{2}^{2}}

near
Z\cap U_{w}
. Consequently,

(33)

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\tilde{v}_{0}(2b_{3}-\frac{1-r_{2}^{2}}{1-\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}-\tilde{v}_{2}^{2}}\tilde{v}_{0})

modulo
\tilde{f}_{0}
and
\tilde{f}_{1}
near
Z\cap U_{w}
, where
\tilde{v}_{0}:=(\tilde{u}_{1}\tilde{v}_{1}+\tilde{u}_{2}\tilde{v}_{2})
. In particular, when
b_{3}\neq 0
, the divisor of
\tilde{f}_{2}
on
\tilde{S}
is defined by
\tilde{v}_{0}
near all points of
Z\cap U_{w}
.

9.5.2
.

Now we compute the divisor of
\tilde{f}_{3}
on
\tilde{S}
. Since
(\tilde{v}_{0},\tilde{v}_{1})
have linearly independent differentials whenever
\tilde{u}_{2}\neq 0
, we may mod out by the relation
\tilde{v}_{2}=(\tilde{v}_{0}-\tilde{u}_{1}\tilde{v}_{1})/\tilde{u}_{2}
as well as by
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
. This yields, after a long computation, that

(34)

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{3}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\tilde{h}_{3,0}}{(1-\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}-\tilde{v}_{2}^{2})\tilde{u}_{2}^{2}}\tilde{v}_{0}+\frac{1}{\tilde{u}_{2}^{2}}((c_{1}\tilde{u}_{1}+c_{2}\tilde{u}_{2})^{2}+(c_{3}\tilde{u}_{2}+c_{1})^{2}+(c_{3}\tilde{u}_{1}-c_{2})^{2})\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}

where
\tilde{h}_{3,0}
is a polynomial in all
\tilde{u}_{i},\tilde{v}_{j},c_{k}
and
r_{3}
.

9.5.3
.

It follows that at each point
P
of
Z\cap U_{w}\cap\{\tilde{u}_{2}\neq 0\}
, the ideal generated by
\tilde{f}_{2},\tilde{f}_{3}
in
\mathcal{O}_{P,\tilde{S}}
is the same as the ideal generated by
\tilde{v}_{0}
and
\tilde{h}_{3,1}\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}
, where

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{3,1}

\displaystyle:=(c_{1}\tilde{u}_{1}+c_{2}\tilde{u}_{2})^{2}+(c_{3}\tilde{u}_{2}+c_{1})^{2}+(c_{3}\tilde{u}_{1}-c_{2})^{2}

Since
\tilde{h}_{3,1}\neq 0
near generic points of
Z
, it follows from identities (
33
) and (
34
) that

\displaystyle(\tilde{f}_{2}|_{\tilde{S}},\tilde{f}_{3}|_{\tilde{S}})_{Z}

\displaystyle=(\tilde{v}_{0}|_{\tilde{S}},\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}|_{\tilde{S}})_{Z}=2

as long as
b_{3}\neq 0
(since
\tilde{v}_{0},\tilde{v}_{1}
generate the ideal of
Z
in
\mathcal{O}_{P,\tilde{S}}
for generic
P\in\tilde{S}\cap Z
, the above equality can be regarded as a (rather trivial) application of
8.2.1
). Identities (
31
) and (
32
) then imply that

(35)

\displaystyle(F_{0},\ldots,F_{4})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=4\times 2=8

9.6
.

It remains to compute the sum
\sum_{P\in Z}(F_{0},\ldots,F_{4})_{P}^{\ord}
from the right hand side of (
30
). Write the scheme theoretic intersection of
F_{0},\ldots,F_{3}
as
mZ+C
. Then

(36)

\displaystyle\sum_{P\in Z}(F_{0},\ldots,F_{4})_{P}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in Z}\ord_{P}(F_{4}|_{C})

(see e.g.
Example

7.8
).

9.6.1
.

First we consider the case that
P\in U_{w,2}:=U_{w}\cap\{\tilde{u}_{2}\neq 0\}
. The arguments from
Section

9.5
imply that

•

if
b_{3}\neq 0
, then
C\cap U_{w,2}
is the scheme theoretic intersection of
\tilde{S}
and
V(\tilde{v}_{0},\tilde{h}_{3,1})
, where
\tilde{h}_{3,1}:=(c_{1}\tilde{u}_{1}+c_{2}\tilde{u}_{2})^{2}+(c_{3}\tilde{u}_{2}+c_{1})^{2}+(c_{3}\tilde{u}_{1}-c_{2})^{2}
, and

•

the restriction of
\tilde{f}_{4}
to
C\cap U_{w,2}
equals (the restriction of)
\tilde{h}_{4,1}\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}/\tilde{u}_{2}^{2}
, where
\tilde{h}_{4,1}:=(d_{1}\tilde{u}_{1}+d_{2}\tilde{u}_{2})^{2}+(d_{3}\tilde{u}_{2}+d_{1})^{2}+(d_{3}\tilde{u}_{1}-d_{2})^{2}
.

Since
U_{w,2}\cap Z\cap C=U_{w,2}\cap V(\tilde{z},\tilde{v}_{1},\tilde{v}_{2},\tilde{u}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{u}_{2}^{2}+1,\tilde{h}_{3,1})
, for generic
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
, there are precisely
2\times 2=4
points in
U_{w,2}\cap Z\cap C
due to Bézout’s theorem. If
d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}
are generic, then
\tilde{h}_{4,1}
is nonzero at each of these points, so that

\displaystyle\sum_{P\in Z\cap U_{w,2}}\ord_{P}(F_{4}|_{C})

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in Z\cap C\cap U_{w,2}}\ord_{P}((\tilde{h}_{4,1}\tilde{v}_{1}^{2})|_{C})

(37)

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in Z\cap C\cap U_{w,2}}\ord_{P}(\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}|_{C})=4\times 2=8

9.6.2
.

It remains to consider the points in
Z\setminus U_{w,2}=V(z,v_{1},v_{2},u_{2},u_{1}^{2}+w^{2})\cup V(z,v_{1},v_{2},w,u_{1}^{2}+u_{2}^{2})
. First we consider the case that
P\in V(z,v_{1},v_{2},u_{2},u_{1}^{2}+w^{2})
. Running the same computations from
Section

9.5.2
with the substitution
\tilde{v}_{1}=(\tilde{v}_{0}-\tilde{u}_{2}\tilde{v}_{2})/\tilde{u}_{1}
(instead of the substitution of
\tilde{v}_{2}
) shows that near
Z
on
\tilde{S}\cap\{\tilde{u}_{1}\neq 0\}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{3}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\tilde{h}_{3,1}}{\tilde{u}_{1}^{2}}\tilde{v}_{1}^{2}

modulo (the ideal generated by)
\tilde{v}_{0}
. If
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
are generic, then
\tilde{h}_{3,1}
is nonzero when
\tilde{u}_{2}=0
. It follows that for each
P\in V(z,v_{1},v_{2},u_{2},u_{1}^{2}+w^{2})
,

is the

\bigcap_{i=0}^{3}F_{i}
near

; in particular,

(38)

\displaystyle\sum_{P\in V(z,v_{1},v_{2},u_{2},u_{1}^{2}+w^{2})}\ord_{P}(F_{4}|_{C})

\displaystyle=0

9.6.3
.

Now we treat the case that
P\in V(z,v_{1},v_{2},w,u_{1}^{2}+u_{2}^{2})
. Consider the chart
U_{2}:=\mathbb{P}^{5}\setminus V(u_{2})
with coordinates

. The polynomials defining

are:

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{0}

\displaystyle:=z^{\prime}w^{\prime}-v^{\prime}_{1}+u^{\prime}_{1}v^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle:=w^{\prime 2}+u^{\prime 2}_{1}+1-(v^{\prime 2}_{1}+v^{\prime 2}_{2}+z^{\prime 2})

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle:=w^{\prime 2}+(u^{\prime}_{1}+b_{3}v^{\prime}_{1})^{2}+(1+b_{3}v^{\prime}_{2})^{2}-r_{2}^{2}(v^{\prime 2}_{1}+v^{\prime 2}_{2}+z^{\prime 2})

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{3}

\displaystyle:=(w^{\prime}-(c_{2}v^{\prime}_{1}-c_{1}v^{\prime}_{2}))^{2}+(u^{\prime}_{1}+c_{3}v^{\prime}_{1}-c_{1}z^{\prime})^{2}+(1+c_{3}v^{\prime}_{2}-c_{2}z^{\prime})^{2}-r_{3}^{2}(v^{\prime 2}_{1}+v^{\prime 2}_{2}+z^{\prime 2})

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{4}

\displaystyle:=(w^{\prime}-(d_{2}v^{\prime}_{1}-d_{1}v^{\prime}_{2}))^{2}+(u^{\prime}_{1}+d_{3}v^{\prime}_{1}-d_{1}z^{\prime})^{2}+(1+d_{3}v^{\prime}_{2}-d_{2}z^{\prime})^{2}-r_{4}^{2}(v^{\prime 2}_{1}+v^{\prime 2}_{2}+z^{\prime 2})

Let
S^{\prime}:=V(f^{\prime}_{0},f^{\prime}_{1})\subseteq U_{2}
. Computations similar to those in

show that the following hold in

:

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle=(1-r_{2}^{2})z^{\prime 2}+2b_{3}u^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}z^{\prime}-2b_{3}v^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime 2}+\cdots

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{3}

\displaystyle=-2(c_{2}+c_{1}u^{\prime}_{1})z^{\prime}-2(c_{2}u^{\prime}_{1}-c_{1})v^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}+(c_{2}u^{\prime}_{1}-c_{1})^{2}v^{\prime 2}_{2}+\cdots

Since near

9.7
.

Combining equations (

), (

), (
