), (
37
), (
38
), (
39
) we obtain

\displaystyle(f_{0},\ldots,f_{4})_{\mathbbm{k}^{5}}^{iso}

\displaystyle=32-8-8-0-4=12

when
b_{3},c_{i},d_{j}
are generic. This completes the computation of the number of tangent lines to four generic spheres in
\mathbbm{k}^{3}
(when
\character(\mathbbm{k})=0
). Note that the above arguments did
not
require any
r_{i}
to be generic, i.e. we showed that the bound of 12 tangent lines can be achieved even if the radii of the spheres are fixed (and nonzero).

10.
Inductive “solutions” to the affine Bézout problem

In
Sections

7
to
9
we described a few scenarios in which ordered intersection multiplicity can be interpreted in terms of intersection multiplicities of regular sequences. However, as pointed out in
Section

8.3.6
, those techniques are
not
always applicable. In this section we present some other interpretations in terms of intersection multiplicities of regular sequences which, in theory, are more generally available, but seem rather non-constructive (those from
Sections

10.1
and
10.2
) or complicated to obtain (the interpretation from
Section

10.3
). Each of these interpretations, say interpretation
\mathscr{I}
, gives rise to a (similarly non-constructive or complicated) algorithm for counting isolated solutions of (square) systems of polynomials as follows:

(1)

start with an
upper
bound which is exact in a generic scenario (e.g. Bézout’s bound or some other Bernstein-Kushnirenko type estimate
[
Mon21
, Theorems X.4 and X.7]
when the ambient space is
\mathbbm{k}^{n}
or
\mathbb{P}^{n}
); if the system is
non-degenerate
, i.e. there are no “extra intersections” (e.g. “intersections at infinity” or non-isolated points in the intersection), then this bound gives the correct result;

(2)

otherwise express the corresponding ordered multiplicities in terms of usual intersection multiplicities of regular sequences using interpretation
\mathscr{I}
;

(3)

compute Bernstein-Kushnirenko type
lower
bounds for the latter intersection multiplicity in terms of associated Newton diagrams (using
8.1.6
and
8.2.1
); if the system is non-degenerate, then these estimates are exact;

(4)

otherwise there are excess intersections in corresponding toric blow-ups at the origin, and repeat the process starting from step (
2
) to estimate the corresponding ordered intersection multiplicities.

10.1
.
An “asymptotic” interpretation

Consider the set up from
Section

6.1
. Given
\vec{g}=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\allowbreak\in\prod_{i}L_{i}
and
\vec{r}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, write
\tilde{X}(\vec{g};\vec{r})
for the “weighted blow up” of
\bar{X}\times\mathbbm{k}
considered in
Section

6.3
, i.e. the closure in
\bar{X}\times\mathbbm{k}\times\mathbb{P}^{n}(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},1)
of the graph of the map

\displaystyle\bar{X}\times\mathbbm{k}\ni(x,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto[\frac{f_{1}}{g_{1}}(x):\cdots:\frac{f_{n}}{g_{n}}(x):t]\in\mathbb{P}^{n}(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},1)

As in
Section

6.3
denote the (weighted) homogeneous coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{n}(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},1)
by
[w_{1}:\cdots:w_{n+1}]
, and let
u_{i}:=w_{i}/(w_{n+1})^{r_{i}}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, be the standard affine coordinates on
\tilde{U}_{n+1}:=\tilde{X}(\vec{g};\vec{r})\setminus V(w_{n+1})
. The following result shows that under (
condition

r

.

/
r

.
+

>
>
1

) the ordered intersection multiplicity can be interpreted as a sum of usual intersection multiplicities at nonsingular points on the normalization of
\tilde{U}_{n+1}
.

Proposition 10.1.1
.

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional affine variety,
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\in\mathbbm{k}[X]
, and for each
i=1,\ldots,n
, let
L_{i}
be a base-point free (finite dimensional) vector subspace of
\mathbbm{k}[X]
containing
f_{i}
. Let
Z
be a distinguished component for (the ordered intersection of the hypersurfaces defined by)
V(f_{1}),\ldots,V(f_{n})
(
2.8
), and
\check{Z}
be the intersection of
Z
with the union of all other distinguished components
Z^{\prime}
with
\codim(Z^{\prime})\geq\codim(Z)
. Then for each nonempty open subset
Z^{*}
of
Z
, there are
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
, and a nonempty open subset
L^{*}
of
\prod_{i}L_{i}
such that under (
condition

r

.

/
r

.
+

>
ν

), for each
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\in L^{*}
, the normalization
\tilde{U}^{\prime}_{n+1}
of
\tilde{U}_{n+1}
is nonsingular at each point of
V(t,u_{1}-1,\ldots,u_{n}-1)
, and

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\in\tilde{U}^{\prime}_{n+1}\cap\pi^{-1}(Z^{*}\setminus\check{Z})}(t,u_{1}-1,\ldots,u_{n}-1)_{u}

where
\pi:\tilde{U}^{\prime}_{n+1}\to\bar{X}
is the natural projection.

Proof.

This follows from arguments as in the proof of
6.3.1
and
6.3.4
and the observation that a normal variety is nonsingular in codimension one.
∎

10.2
.
An expression in positive characteristic

Cowsik and Nori
[
CN78
]
showed that affine curves are set theoretic complete intersections in positive characteristic. It follows that in positive characteristic each “summand” of
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}
can be represented as an intersection multiplicity up to a multiple; more precisely, for each
\vec{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho})
, consider the curves
C_{\vec{i},\vec{g}}=V(I^{(\rho-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}},g_{i^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,g_{i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}})
considered in
Section

7.5
, where
i^{\prime}_{j}
are as usual the elements of
\{1,\ldots,n\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}\}
, and
g_{i^{\prime}_{j}}
are generic elements from
\mathcal{L}_{i^{\prime}_{j}}
. By Cowsik and Nori’s theorem, for each irreducible component
C_{\vec{i},\vec{g};k}
of
C_{\vec{i},\vec{g}}
, one can find
h_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};k,l}
,
l=1,\ldots,n-1
, such that set theoretically

\displaystyle C_{\vec{i},\vec{g};k}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{l=1,\ldots,n-1}V(h_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};k,l})

Proposition 10.2.1
.

With the above set up,

\displaystyle\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})\deg_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{n-\rho}}(Z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{1}{n_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};k}}\sum_{z\in Z}(h_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};k,1},\ldots,h_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};k,n-1},f_{i_{\rho}})_{z}

\displaystyle(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})_{Z}^{\ord}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho},k\\
z\in Z\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};k}}(h_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};k,1},\ldots,h_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};k,n-1},f_{i_{\rho}})_{z}

Proof.

This follows immediately from
7.6
.
∎

10.3
.
Expression for irreducible crossing divisors

Given a Cartier divisor
D
on
X
, we say that
D
is an
irreducible crossing divisor
if for each
z\in\supp(D)
, there is an open neighborhood
U
of
z
in
X
, and regular functions
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}
on
U
such that

(1)

D
is represented on
U
by a monomial in
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}
,

(2)

V(h_{k_{1}},\ldots,h_{k_{m}})\cap U
is irreducible for each choice of
k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}
.

We say that
D
is a
simplicial crossing divisor
if the following stronger version of property (
2
) is satisfied:

(2
′
)

if
k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}
are pairwise distinct, then
V(h_{k_{1}},\ldots,h_{k_{m}})\cap U
is irreducible of
codimension

m
if
m\leq n
, and empty if
m>n
.

We now show that the ordered intersection multiplicity of
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
along a variety
Z
can be expressed in terms of the usual intersection multiplicities if
\bigcup\supp(F_{i})
is the support of an irreducible crossing divisor near generic points of
Z
. Since a strict normal crossing divisor is a simplicial crossing divisor, and in particular, an irreducible crossing divisor, this gives another inductive route to the affine Bézout problem when resolution of singularities is available (e.g. in characteristic zero).

Proposition 10.3.1
.

Let
Z
be an irreducible component of
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
(as defined in
2.7
) such that
Z\not\subseteq\sing(X)
. Assume there is an open subset
\tilde{U}
of
X
such that
\tilde{U}\cap Z\neq\emptyset
, and
\tilde{U}\cap\sum_{i}F_{i}
is an irreducible crossing divisor. Then there is an open subset
U
of
\tilde{U}
and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\in\mathbbm{k}[U]
such that

(1)

U\cap Z\neq\emptyset
,

(2)

each
F_{i}
is represented by
f_{i}
on
U
, and

(3)

there are divisors
f_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j,s_{j}}
of
f_{i_{j}}
in
\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}
,
j=1,\ldots,\rho
, such that

\displaystyle\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{\rho}}(f_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};1,s_{1}},\ldots,f_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};\rho,s_{\rho}})_{Z}

If
\sum_{i}F_{i}
is a simplicial crossing divisor, then there is only one possible choice of
s_{j}
for each
j
, and more precisely, there are irreducible elements
f^{\prime}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}\in\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}
and positive integers
\alpha_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}
,
j=1,\ldots,\rho
, such that for each
j
,

(4)

up to multiplication by a unit in
\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}
,
f_{i_{j}}
equals
(f^{\prime}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j})^{\alpha_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j}}
times a monomial in
f^{\prime}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};j+1},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};\rho}
; in particular,

(5)

f_{i_{\rho}}
equals
(f^{\prime}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};\rho})^{\alpha_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};\rho}}
times a unit in
\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}
, and

\displaystyle\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})

\displaystyle=((f^{\prime}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};1})^{\alpha_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};1}},\ldots,(f^{\prime}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};\rho})^{\alpha_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho};\rho}})_{Z}

Proof.

Fix an open subset
U
of
\tilde{U}
such that
U\cap Z\neq\emptyset
, and each
F_{i}
is represented by some
f_{i}\in\mathbbm{k}[U]
, and there are regular functions
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}
on
U
such that

(i)

\sum_{i}F_{i}
is represented on
U
by a monomial in
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}
, and

(ii)

V(h_{k_{1}},\ldots,h_{k_{m}})\cap U
is irreducible for each choice of
k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}
.

Choosing
U
sufficiently small if necessary, we may also assume that

(iii)

each
h_{i}
is an irreducible element in
\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}
, and

(iv)

Z\cap U\subseteq V(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})
.

Now consider the construction of the ideals
I^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
from
2.11
. Since
Z\not\subseteq\sing(X)
, the Auslander–Buchsbaum theorem implies that
\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}
is a UFD, so that

(v)

each
f_{i}
is a monomial in
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}
.

Since all sets of the form
V(h_{s_{1}},\ldots,h_{s_{m}})\cap U
are irreducible, this implies that for each
j=1,\ldots,\rho
,

(vi)

each primary component of
I^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\cap U
is of the form
V(h_{s_{1}}^{m_{1}},\ldots,h_{s_{j}}^{m_{j}})
where
s_{1},\ldots,s_{j}
are pairwise distinct, and
h_{s_{l}}^{m_{l}}
are divisors of
f_{i_{l}}
,
l=1,\ldots,j
.

The preceding observation, applied to
j=\rho
, implies that

\displaystyle\len(\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}/I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{Z,X})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{\rho}}(h_{s_{1}}^{m_{1}},\ldots,h_{s_{\rho}}^{m_{\rho}})_{Z}

where the sum is over all
s_{1},\ldots,s_{\rho}
such that
h_{s_{1}}^{m_{1}},\ldots,h_{s_{\rho}}^{m_{\rho}}
generate a primary component of
I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
. This proves the first assertion of
10.3.1
. Now since
Z
is an irreducible component of
V(I^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}})
, observations
(iv)
and
(vi)
imply that

(vii)

Z=V(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})
.

Replacing
U
by a smaller open subset if necessary, we may assume that
Z\cap U
is the only irreducible component of
Z^{(\rho)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\cap U=Z^{(\rho-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\cap V(f_{i_{\rho}})\cap U
. Now assume
\sum_{i}F_{i}
is a simplicial crossing divisor. Then

(viii)

\rho=\codim(Z)=\codim(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})=k
.

On the other hand, it follows from the definition of the
I^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
that

(ix)

if
h_{s_{1}}^{m_{1}},\ldots,h_{s_{j}}^{m_{j}}
generate a primary component of
I^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
, then none of the
h_{s_{l}}
divides
\prod_{j^{\prime}>j}f_{i_{j^{\prime}}}
.

Since
\rho=k
, applying the preceding observation to
j=\rho-1
, then to
j=\rho-2
, and so on up to
j=1
, it follows after a renumbering of the
h_{s}
if necessary that for each
j=1,\ldots,\rho
,

(x)

up to multiplication by a unit in
\mathcal{O}_{Z,X}
,
f_{i_{j}}
equals
h_{j}^{m_{j}}
times a monomial in
h_{j+1},\ldots,h_{\rho}
;

(xi)

I^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
is generated by
h_{1}^{m_{1}},\ldots,h_{j}^{m_{j}}
.

This completes the proof of
10.3.1
.
∎

Part III
Appendix

Appendix A
Miscellaneous algebraic results used in the article

Lemma A.1
.

Let
C
be a (possibly non-reduced) curve,
z\in C
, and
t
be a non zero-divisor and non-unit in
\mathcal{O}_{z,C}
, so that
m:=\len(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{z,C}/t\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{z,C})
is positive and finite. If
h\in\mathcal{O}_{z,C}
is nilpotent, then
h^{m}=0
.

Proof.

Since
t
is a non zero-divisor in
\mathcal{O}_{z,C}
, it follows from standard commutative algebra that there is a
\mathbbm{k}[[t]]
-module isomorphism

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{z,C}\cong\mathbbm{k}[[t]]^{m}

(see e.g.
[
Mon21
, Proof of Proposition C.6]
). Since
t
is a non zero-divisor, it follows that

\displaystyle(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{z,C})_{t}\cong\mathbbm{k}((t))^{m}

as a vector space over the field
\mathbbm{k}((t))
of Laurent series in
t
. Now pick the smallest
s\geq 1
such that
h^{s}=0
. We claim that
1,h,\ldots,h^{s-1}
are linearly independent over
\mathbbm{k}((t))
. Indeed, otherwise there is a relation of the form

\displaystyle h^{s^{\prime}}(\phi_{0}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{s^{\prime\prime}}h^{j}\phi_{j}(t))=0

with
0\leq s^{\prime}<s
and each
\phi_{j}\in\mathbbm{k}[[t]]
. However, since
t
is a non zero-divisor and
h
is nilpotent, it follows that
\phi_{0}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{s^{\prime\prime}}h^{j}\phi_{j}(t)
is also a non zero-divisor. Then
h^{s^{\prime}}=0
, contradicting the choice of
s
. This proves the claim. It follows that
s\leq m
, as required.
∎

Proposition A.2
.

Let
I
be an ideal of pure codimension
k
in a Noetherian ring
R
. Let
f\in R
and
I^{\prime}:=IR_{f}\cap R
.

(1)

If
f\in\sqrt{I}
, then
I^{\prime}=R
.

(2)

If
f\not\in\sqrt{I}
, then
I^{\prime}
has pure codimension
k
and
I^{\prime}+fR
has pure codimension
k+1
in
R
. Moreover, for each codimension
k+1
prime
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
of
R
,

(40)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\len(R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}/(I^{\prime}R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}+fR_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}))&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{p}\subseteq\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\\
\codim(\mathfrak{p})=k\end{subarray}}\len(R_{\mathfrak{p}}/I^{\prime}R_{\mathfrak{p}})\len(R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}/(\mathfrak{p}R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}+fR_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}))\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\not\in\mathfrak{p}\subseteq\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\\
\codim(\mathfrak{p})=k\end{subarray}}\len(R_{\mathfrak{p}}/IR_{\mathfrak{p}})\len(R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}/(\mathfrak{p}R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}+fR_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}))\end{split}

Proof.

The first assertion is immediate. So assume
f\not\in\sqrt{I}
. Then
I^{\prime}+fR
has pure codimension
k+1
in
R
due to Krull’s principal ideal theorem. So for each prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
associated to
I
, we have

\displaystyle\codim(I^{\prime}+fR)=k+1>k=\codim(\mathfrak{p})

so that

\displaystyle I^{\prime}+fR\not\subseteq\mathfrak{p}

In particular, if
f\in\mathfrak{p}
, then
I^{\prime}\not\subseteq\mathfrak{p}
, so that
I^{\prime}R_{\mathfrak{p}}=R_{\mathfrak{p}}
. The second equality of (
40
) then follows from the following observation that

\displaystyle I^{\prime}R_{\mathfrak{p}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}R_{\mathfrak{p}}&\text{if}\ f\in\mathfrak{p},\\
IR_{\mathfrak{p}}&\text{if}\ f\not\in\mathfrak{p}.\end{cases}

Fix a codimension
k+1
prime
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
of
R
containing
I^{\prime}+fR
. An application of
[
Ful98
, Lemma A.2.7]
with
M=A=R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}/I^{\prime}R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}
shows that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{p}\subseteq\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\\
\codim(\mathfrak{p})=k\end{subarray}}\len(R_{\mathfrak{p}}/I^{\prime}R_{\mathfrak{p}})\len(R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}/(\mathfrak{p}R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}+fR_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle=e_{R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}/I^{\prime}R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}}(f)

where
e_{\cdot}(\cdot)
is the “multiplicity” defined by

\displaystyle e_{A}(a)

\displaystyle:=\len(A/aA)-\len(0:a)

However, it follows from the definition of
I^{\prime}
that
f
is a non zero-divisor in
R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}/I^{\prime}R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}
, and consequently,

\displaystyle e_{R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}/I^{\prime}R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}}(f)

\displaystyle=\len(R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}/(I^{\prime}R_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}+fR_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}))

as required.
∎

Appendix B
A result about completions of local rings at a subvariety

The goal of this section is to prove
Lemma

B.2
which is used in
Appendix

C
to prove
6.4.2
.

Theorem B.1
.

Let
X
be an irreducible affine variety,
Z
be an irreducible subvariety of
X
. Let
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q},f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in\mathbbm{k}[X]
such that

(1)

\mathbbm{k}[Z]
is algebraic over
\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1}|_{Z},\ldots,\phi_{q}|_{Z}]
,

(2)

Z
is set-theoretically the closed subset
V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})
of
X
.

Then

(i)

\mathbbm{k}(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q})[[f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}]]
is a subring of
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{Z,X}
, and there is a nonzero element
\phi\in\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}]
such that
\mathbbm{k}[X]
is integral over
\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}]_{\phi}[[f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}]]
in
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{Z,X}
.

(ii)

If
\mathbbm{k}[Z]
is integral over
\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1}|_{Z},\ldots,\phi_{q}|_{Z}]
, then

(a)

we can take
\phi=1
in the preceding sentence, i.e.
\mathbbm{k}[X]
is integral over
\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}][[f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}]]
in
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{Z,X}
,

(b)

in addition, the completion
\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}
of
\mathbbm{k}[X]
with respect to the prime ideal of
Z
is integral over the subring
\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}][[f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}]]
.

Proof.

Assume
X
is a subvariety of
\mathbbm{k}^{N}
with coordinates
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
. We abuse the notation to use
\phi_{i}
(respectively,
f_{j}
) also for a representative of
\phi_{i}
(respectively,
f_{j}
) in
\mathbbm{k}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}]
. Let
J
be the ideal of
\mathbbm{k}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}]
generated by
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
, so that

\displaystyle I(Z)=\sqrt{I(X)+J}

For each
i
,
x_{i}|_{Z}
is algebraic over
\mathbbm{k}(\phi_{1}|_{Z},\ldots,\phi_{q}|_{Z})
. Taking a power of the minimal equation of
x_{i}|_{Z}
over
\mathbbm{k}(\phi_{1}|_{Z},\ldots,\phi_{q}|_{Z})
if necessary, we have that

\displaystyle a_{i,0}(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q})x_{i}^{d_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{d_{i}}a_{i,j}(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q})\in(I(X)+J)

for some
a_{i,0},\ldots,a_{i,d_{i}}\in\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}]
with
a_{i,d_{i}}\neq 0
. It follows that

\displaystyle x_{i}^{d_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{d_{i}}\frac{a_{i,j}}{a_{i,0}}x_{i}^{d_{i}-j}=\frac{1}{a_{i,0}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{r}g_{i,j}f_{j}+g^{\prime}_{i}\right)

where
g_{i,1},\ldots,g_{i,r},g^{\prime}_{i}
are polynomials in
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
and
g^{\prime}_{i}\in I(X)
. Let

\displaystyle\phi:=\prod_{i=1}^{N}a_{i,0}\in\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}]

and
\mathcal{A}
be the (finite) set of all
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{N})\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{N}
such that
\alpha_{i}<d_{i}
for each
i
.

Claim B.1.1
.

Each
h\in\mathbbm{k}[X]
can be represented in
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{Z,X}
as a
\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}]_{\phi}[[f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}]]
-linear combination of
x^{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

Given
h\in\mathbbm{k}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}]
, replacing each
(x_{i})^{d_{i}}
using the above equation and repeating as many times as necessary, one has that

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}b_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\phi^{m_{1}}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{r}f_{j}h_{j}+h^{\prime}_{1}\right)

where the
b_{\alpha}\in\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}]_{\phi}
,
h_{1},\ldots,h_{r},h^{\prime}_{1}
are polynomials in
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
and
h^{\prime}_{1}\in I(X)
. Then continue the same process with each
h_{j}
to yield

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}b_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{1}{\phi^{m_{1}}}f_{j}\left(\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}b_{\alpha,j}x^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\phi^{m_{2}}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{r}f_{k}h_{j,k}+h^{\prime}_{1,j}\right)\right)+\frac{h^{\prime}_{1}}{\phi^{m_{1}}}

which can be rewritten as

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\left(b_{\alpha}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{b_{\alpha,j}}{\phi^{m_{1}}}f_{j}\right)x^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\phi^{m_{1}+m_{2}}}\left(\sum_{j,k}f_{j}f_{k}h_{j,k}+h^{\prime}_{2}\right)

This can be continued with

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\left(b_{\alpha}+\sum_{j}\frac{b_{\alpha,j}}{\phi^{m_{1}}}f_{j}+\sum_{j,k}\frac{b_{\alpha,j,k}}{\phi^{m_{1}+m_{2}}}f_{j}f_{k}\right)x^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\phi^{m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}}}\left(\sum_{j,k,l}f_{j}f_{k}f_{l}h_{j,k,l}+h^{\prime}_{3}\right)

and so on to obtain a sum
\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}c_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}
with
c_{\alpha}\in\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}]_{\phi}[[f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}]]
such that for each
M
,
h|_{X}\equiv\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}(c_{\alpha}x^{\alpha})|_{X}
modulo the ideal in
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{Z,X}
generated by
f^{\alpha}
with
|\alpha|>M
. Since each
f_{j}
is in the maximal ideal of
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{Z,X}
, it follows (e.g. since the intersection of all powers of a proper ideal in a Noetherian ring is zero) that

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}c_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\in\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{Z,X}

This completes the proof.
∎

Now pick
f\in\mathbbm{k}[X]
. Apply
B.1.1
to
h:=fx^{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
:

\displaystyle fx^{\alpha}=\sum_{\beta}c_{\alpha,\beta}x^{\beta}

where each
c_{\alpha,\beta}\in\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}]_{\phi}[[f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}]]
and the sum is over all
\beta\in\mathcal{A}
. Let
M:=|\mathcal{A}|
. Consider the
M\times M
matrix
A
with entry
\delta_{\alpha,\beta}f-c_{\alpha,\beta}
at the cell with index
(\alpha,\beta)
. If
R
is the subring of
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{Z,X}
generated by
\mathbbm{k}[X]
and
\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}]_{\phi}[[f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}]]
then
A
acts on
R^{M}
by multiplication on the left:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\phi_{M}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto A\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\phi_{M}\end{pmatrix}

It follows from the definition of the
c_{\alpha,\beta}
that

\displaystyle A\begin{pmatrix}x^{\alpha_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
x^{\alpha_{M}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{pmatrix}

Multiplying on the left by the matrix of cofactors of
A
yields that
\det(A)x^{\alpha}=0
for each
\alpha
. In particular, taking
\alpha=0
yields that
\det(A)=0
. This completes the proof of assertions
(i)
and
(ii.a)
. For the remaining assertion
(ii.b)
it suffices to show that
B.1.1
continues to hold if we replace
\mathbbm{k}[X]
by
\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}
, since then we can use the determinantal trick to prove integrality. So assume
\phi=1
as in assertion
(ii)
, and pick
h\in\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}
. Assume
h
is the inverse limit of
(h_{n})_{n\geq 0}
, with

\displaystyle h_{n}\equiv h_{m}\mod(I(Z))^{n}

for all
m\geq n
. We are going to construct
c_{\alpha,\beta}\in\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}]
such that
h=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}c_{\alpha,\beta}x^{\alpha}f^{\beta}
, where the
\alpha
vary over
\mathcal{A}
and the
\beta
over
(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{r}
, and
x^{\alpha},f^{\beta}
are short-hands for respectively
\prod_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
and
\prod_{i=1}^{r}f_{i}^{\beta_{i}}
. Fix a monomial ordering

\displaystyle 0=\beta^{0}\prec\beta^{1}\prec\cdots

of
(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{r}
which has “finite depth”, i.e. for each
\beta\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{r}
, there are only finitely many
\beta^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{r}
such that
\beta^{\prime}\preceq\beta
. For each
k\geq 0
, let
J_{k}
be the ideal generated by
\{f^{\beta^{j}}:j\geq k\}
. Fix a set of generators
g_{1},\ldots,g_{s}
of
I(Z)
. Since
\prec
has finite depth, we can pick
0<n_{0}<n_{1}<\cdots
such that for each
\gamma\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{s}
with
|\gamma|:=\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}=n_{k}
, the “
g
monomial”
g^{\gamma}:=\prod_{i}(g_{i})^{\gamma_{i}}
is in
J_{k}
. Due to
B.1.1
,
h_{n_{1}}
has an expression of the form

\displaystyle h_{n_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}c_{n_{1},\alpha,\beta}(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q})x^{\alpha}f^{\beta}

For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
, define

\displaystyle c_{\alpha,\beta^{0}}

\displaystyle:=c_{n_{1},\alpha,\beta^{0}}

For each
n\geq n_{1}
, since
h_{n}-h_{n_{1}}\in(I(z))^{n_{1}}
, by definition of
n_{1}
and using
B.1.1
we can write

\displaystyle h_{n_{2}}-h_{n_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{\beta\succeq\beta^{1}}c_{n_{2},\alpha,\beta}(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q})x^{\alpha}f^{\beta}

For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
, define

\displaystyle c_{\alpha,\beta^{1}}

\displaystyle:=c_{n_{1},\alpha,\beta^{1}}+c_{n_{2},\alpha,\beta^{1}}

In the same way, for each
k
, we write

\displaystyle h_{n_{k+1}}-h_{n_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{\beta\succeq\beta^{k}}c_{n_{k+1},\alpha,\beta}(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q})x^{\alpha}f^{\beta},

\displaystyle c_{\alpha,\beta^{k}}

\displaystyle:=c_{n_{1},\alpha,\beta^{k}}+\cdots+c_{n_{k+1},\alpha,\beta^{k}}

Finally, define

\displaystyle h^{\prime}:=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{k\geq 0}c_{\alpha,\beta^{k}}x^{\alpha}f^{\beta^{k}},

It is straightforward to check that

\displaystyle h^{\prime}

\displaystyle\equiv h_{n_{k}}\mod J_{k}

which implies that
h^{\prime}=h
, as required to complete the proof.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

In the set up of
B.1
, assume
\mathbbm{k}[Z]
is integral over
\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1}|_{Z},\ldots,\phi_{q}|_{Z}]
. Let
s\leq r
and
f\in\mathbbm{k}[X]
be such that
f
does not vanish identically on any irreducible component of
V_{s}:=V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{s})
or equivalently,
f
is not in any minimal prime ideal in
\mathbbm{k}[X]
of the radical of the ideal
\mathfrak{q}_{s}
generated by
f_{1},\ldots,f_{s}
. Then
f
satisfies an identity of the form

\displaystyle f^{d}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\xi_{j}f^{d-j}+\sum_{k=1}^{s}f_{k}\chi_{k}=0

in the completion
\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}
of
\mathbbm{k}[X]
with respect to
I(Z)
, where

(1)

each
\xi_{j}\in\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}][[f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}]]
,

(2)

\xi_{d}
is nonzero and has a monomial in
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})
in which
f_{k}
does not appear for
k\leq s
,

(3)

each
\chi_{k}\in\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}
.

Proof.

By
B.1
,
f
satisfies an integral equation in
\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}
of the form

\displaystyle f^{d}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\xi_{j}f^{d-j}=0

with each
\xi_{j}\in\mathbbm{k}[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}][[f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}]]
. If
\xi_{d}
has a monomial in
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})
in which
f_{k}
does not appear for
k\leq s
, then
Lemma

B.2
clearly holds. So assume either
\xi_{d}=0
or each monomial in
\xi_{d}
contains some
f_{i}
with
i\leq s
. Now, by assumption,
f
is a non zero-divisor in
\mathbbm{k}[X]/\sqrt{\mathfrak{q}_{s}}
(indeed, if
fg\in\sqrt{\mathfrak{q}_{s}}
, then
fg
vanishes identically on every irreducible component of
V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{s}
), and consequently,
g
vanishes identically on every irreducible component of
V(f_{1},\ldots,f_{s}
)). It then follows from the flatness of completion that
f
is also a non zero-divisor in
\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}/\widehat{\sqrt{\mathfrak{q}_{s}}}\cong\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}/\sqrt{\mathfrak{q}_{s}}\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}
. However, by the assumption on
\xi_{d}
, the above integral equation of
f
can be written as

\displaystyle f^{e}(f^{d-e}+\sum_{j=1}^{d-e}\xi_{j}f^{d-e-j})\in\mathfrak{q}_{s}\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}

where
d-e
is the largest index such that
\xi_{d-e}\not\in\mathfrak{q}_{s}\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}
. Since
f
is non zero-divisor in
\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}/\sqrt{\mathfrak{q}_{s}}\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}
, it follows that

\displaystyle f^{d-e}+\sum_{j=1}^{d-e}\xi_{j}f^{d-e-j}\in\sqrt{\mathfrak{q}_{s}}\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}

and consequently,

\displaystyle(f^{d-e}+\sum_{j=1}^{d-e}\xi_{j}f^{d-e-j})^{m}\in\mathfrak{q}_{s}\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}

for some
m\geq 1
. This completes the proof.
∎

Subclaim C.1.1.1

, and a nonempty subset
U^{\prime\prime*}_{k^{\prime}+1}
of
U^{\prime*}_{0}
in place of
U^{\prime\prime*}_{0}
.

By Noether normalization there are
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n-\rho}\in\mathbbm{k}[U^{\prime*}]
such that

(viii)

u_{1}|_{Z},\ldots,u_{n-\rho}|_{Z}
are algebraically independent over
\mathbbm{k}
, and

(ix)

\mathbbm{k}[Z\cap U^{\prime*}]
is integral over
\mathbbm{k}[u_{1}|_{Z},\ldots,u_{n-\rho}|_{Z}]
.

Due to properties
(iv)
and
(ix)
,
Lemma

B.2
applies (with
h_{i}
in place of the
f_{i}
, the
u_{i}
in place of the
\phi_{i}
, and
f=f_{i_{k^{\prime}}}
and

) and implies that
f_{i_{k^{\prime}}}
satisfies an identity in the completion
\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[U^{\prime*}]}
of
\mathbbm{k}[U^{\prime*}]
with respect to the ideal
I(Z)
of
Z
of the form

\displaystyle f_{i_{k^{\prime}}}^{d}+\sum_{e=1}^{d}\xi_{e}f_{i_{k^{\prime}}}^{d-e}+\sum_{k=1}^{k^{\prime}-1}h_{k}\chi_{k}

\displaystyle=0\in\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[U^{\prime*}]}

where

(x)

each
\xi_{e}\in\mathbbm{k}[u_{1},\ldots,u_{n-\rho}][[h_{1},\ldots,h_{\rho}]]
,

(xi)

\xi_{d}
is nonzero and has a monomial in
(h_{1},\ldots,h_{\rho})
in which
h_{k}
does not appear for
k\leq k^{\prime}-1
,

(xii)

each
\chi_{k}\in\widehat{\mathbbm{k}[X]}
.

For each

, let
\alpha_{e}:=(\alpha_{e,1},\ldots,\alpha_{e,\rho})
denote the
lexicographically minimal
element of
\supp(\xi_{d})
(which is the set of all
(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{\rho})
such that the coefficient of
\prod_{k=1}^{\rho}h_{k}^{\beta_{k}}
in
\xi_{e}
is nonzero). Then the above integral equation of
f_{i_{k^{\prime}}}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle f_{i_{k^{\prime}}}^{d}+\sum_{l}(c_{e_{l}}\prod_{k=k^{\prime}}^{\rho}h_{k}^{\alpha_{e_{l},k}})f_{i_{k^{\prime}}}^{d-e_{l}}+\sum_{l}\xi^{\prime}_{e_{l}}f_{i_{k^{\prime}}}^{d-e_{l}}+\sum_{k=1}^{k^{\prime}-1}h_{k}\chi^{\prime}_{k}

\displaystyle=0

where the second (and the third) sum is over all
l
such that
\alpha_{e_{l},k}=0
for
k=1,\ldots,k^{\prime}-1
, and for each such
l
,

(xiii)

c_{e_{l}}\in\mathbbm{k}[u_{1},\ldots,u_{n-\rho}]
is the
coefficient

\prod_{k=1}^{\rho}h_{k}^{\alpha_{e_{l},k}}
in
\xi_{e_{l}}
is nonzero, and

(xiv)

\xi^{\prime}_{e_{l}}:=\xi_{e_{l}}-c_{e_{l}}\prod_{k=1}^{\rho}h_{k}^{\alpha_{e_{l},k}}
.

Since
u_{1}|_{Z},\ldots,u_{n}|_{Z}
are algebraically independent over
\mathbbm{k}
, none of the
c_{e_{l}}
vanishes identically on
Z
. Consequently,
U^{\prime\prime*}_{k^{\prime}}:=U^{\prime\prime*}_{k^{\prime}+1}\setminus V(\prod_{l}c_{e_{l}})
has a nonempty intersection with
Z
(where
U^{\prime\prime*}_{k^{\prime}+1}
is from the inductive hypothesis). It then follows from the inductive hypothesis and property
(v)
that if
r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
C(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n};r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})
centered at
(U^{\prime\prime*}_{k^{\prime}}\cap Z)\times\{0\}
satisfy condition (

⁢

0

(
ν
)

) for
\nu\gg 1
, then there must be two distinct

, such that

\displaystyle\ord_{B}(f_{i_{k^{\prime}}}^{d-e_{l}}\prod_{k=k^{\prime}}^{\rho}h_{k}^{\alpha_{e_{l},k}})

\displaystyle=\ord_{B}(f_{i_{k^{\prime}}}^{d-e_{m}}\prod_{k=k^{\prime}}^{\rho}h_{k}^{\alpha_{e_{m},k}})

and in addition,

Claim C.2

(
6.4.2
)
.

There is a nonempty open subset
Z^{*}_{0}
of
Z
and
\nu^{*}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that if
z\in Z^{*}_{0}
and
g_{1},\ldots,g_{n},r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}
satisfy (

⁢

0

(
ν
)

) with
\nu\geq\nu^{*}_{0}
, then

(1)

for each
j
,
1\leq j\leq\rho
,

\displaystyle I^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C}\subseteq t^{r_{i_{j}}}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C}

In addition,
Z^{*}_{0}
does not change (but
\nu^{*}_{0}
possibly changes) if the
f_{i}
are replaced by
(f_{i})^{m_{i}}
for
m_{i}\geq 1
.

Pick
i^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}\}
. Recall that
i_{1}=i^{*}
, where
i^{*}
is the smallest index such that
F_{i^{*}}
is not identically zero.

(2)

If
i^{\prime}<i^{*}
, then
g_{i^{\prime}}|_{C}\equiv 0\in t\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C}
.

(3)

Assume
i^{\prime}>i^{*}
. Pick the largest
j
such that
i_{j}<i^{\prime}
. Then
f_{i^{\prime}}\in\sqrt{I^{\prime(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}}
. If
f_{i^{\prime}}\in I^{\prime(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
, then
g_{i^{\prime}}\in t\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C}
.

Proof.

We start with the proof of assertion (
1
). Fix
\phi_{j}\in I^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C}
. Then
\phi_{j}=\phi^{\prime}_{j-1}+f_{i_{j}}\psi_{j}
for some
\phi^{\prime}_{j-1}\in I^{\prime(j-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C}
and
\psi_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C}
. However, then

\displaystyle h^{\prime\prime}_{j-1}\phi_{j}

\displaystyle=\phi_{j-1}+h^{\prime\prime}_{j-1}f_{i_{j}}\psi_{j}

where
h^{\prime\prime}_{j-1}
is from
Lemma

C.1
and
\phi_{j-1}:=h^{\prime\prime}_{j-1}\phi^{\prime}_{j-1}\in I^{\prime(j-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}I^{\prime\prime(j-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\subseteq I^{(j-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}
.
Lemma

C.1
implies that for each branch
B
of
C
at
(z,0)
, there are integers
m_{j-1,B},n_{j-1,B}
such that

\displaystyle\ord_{B}(h^{\prime\prime}_{j-1})=\frac{m_{j-1,B}}{n_{j-1,B}}r_{i_{j}}\ord_{B}(t)

Claim C.2.1
.

There are invertible elements
\xi_{j-1,B,k}\in\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C}
and an integer
m^{\prime}_{j-1}
such that

\displaystyle(t^{r_{i_{j}}m_{j-1,B}N_{j-1,B}}+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{j-1,B}}\xi_{j-1,B,k}t^{r_{i_{j}}m_{j-1,B}(N_{j-1,B}-k)}(h^{\prime\prime}_{j-1})^{kn_{j-1,B}})^{m^{\prime}_{j-1}}

\displaystyle=0\in\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C}

Proof.

Let
C^{\prime}
be the (unique) reduced curve with support equal to
\supp(C)
. Let
\pi:\tilde{C}\to C^{\prime}
be a desingularization of
C^{\prime}
, and
\tilde{z}_{B}
be the center of
B
on
\tilde{C}
. Since
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{z}_{B},\tilde{C}}
is integral over
\pi^{*}(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C^{\prime}})
, we may take an integral equation of
t^{m_{j-1,B}r_{i_{j}}}/(h^{\prime\prime}_{j-1})^{n_{j-1,B}}
over
\pi^{*}(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C^{\prime}})
and clear out the denominators to obtain an identity of the following form in
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{z}_{B},\tilde{C}}
:

\displaystyle t^{r_{i_{j}}m_{j-1,B}N_{j-1,B}}+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{j-1,B}}\pi^{*}(\xi_{j-1,B,k})t^{r_{i_{j}}m_{j-1,B}(N_{j-1,B}-k)}(h^{\prime\prime}_{j-1})^{kn_{j-1,B}}

\displaystyle=0

Due to exactness of completions (i.e. the injectivity of the map
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C^{\prime}}\to\prod_{B}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{z}_{B},\tilde{C}}
), it then follows that the product over all
B
of the left hand side of the above equation is zero in
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C^{\prime}}
. Since the kernel of the natural map from
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C}\to\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C^{\prime}}
is nilpotent, it becomes zero in
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C}
after raising to a power, as required to prove the claim.
∎

Claim C.2.2
.

The total number of branches
B
, and all
m_{j-1,B},n_{j-1,B}
, and
m^{\prime}_{j-1}
can be bounded above by an integer which is independent of
\nu^{*}_{0}
and
B
.

Proof.

The total number of branches
B
is clearly
\leq(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})
.
Lemma

C.1
implies that each
m_{j-1,B},n_{j-1,B}
can be chosen to be bounded above by a quantity independent of
\nu^{*}_{0}
and
B
. On the other hand,
Lemma

A.1
implies that
m^{\prime}_{j-1}
is bounded by above
\ord_{B}(t)
. Since
\ord_{B}(t)\leq(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})
, this completes the proof.
∎

C.2.3
.

It follows that for each
j=2,\ldots,\rho
, there is an identity of the form

\displaystyle t^{Nr_{i_{j}}}-\sum_{k=1}^{N}\xi_{j-1,k}t^{(N-k)r_{i_{j}}}(h^{\prime\prime}_{j-1})^{\alpha_{j-1,k}}\equiv 0\in\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C}

where
N
is independent of
\nu^{*}_{0}
and
B
, and each
\alpha_{j-1,k}
is
positive
. Then in
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C}
one has

\displaystyle t^{Nr_{i_{j}}}\phi_{j}

\displaystyle=t^{Nr_{i_{j}}}(\phi^{\prime}_{j-1}+f_{i_{j}}\psi_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\xi_{j-1,k}t^{(N-k)r_{i_{j}}}(h^{\prime\prime}_{j-1})^{\alpha_{j-1,k}}\phi^{\prime}_{j-1}+t^{Nr_{i_{j}}}f_{i_{j}}\psi_{j}

\displaystyle=\chi_{j-1}\phi_{j-1}+t^{Nr_{i_{j}}+r_{i_{j}}}g_{i_{j}}\psi_{j}

where

\displaystyle\chi_{j-1}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\xi_{j-1,k}t^{(N-k)r_{i_{j}}}(h^{\prime\prime}_{j-1})^{\alpha_{j-1,k}-1}\in\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C},\ \text{and}

\displaystyle\phi_{j-1}

\displaystyle:=h^{\prime\prime}_{j-1}\phi^{\prime}_{j-1}\in I^{(j-1)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}

C.2.4
.

Repeating the arguments of the preceding paragraph for
j-1,j-2,\ldots,2
yields, with some
N^{\prime}\geq N
, that

\displaystyle t^{N^{\prime}\sum_{k=2}^{j}r_{i_{k}}}\phi_{j}

\displaystyle=t^{N^{\prime}(\sum_{k=2}^{j}r_{i_{k}})+r_{i_{j}}}g_{i_{j}}\psi_{j}+\chi_{j-1}t^{N^{\prime}(\sum_{k=2}^{j-1}r_{i_{k}})+r_{i_{j-1}}}g_{i_{j-1}}\psi_{j-1}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\cdots+(\prod_{k=2}^{j-1}\chi_{k})t^{N^{\prime}r_{i_{2}}+r_{i_{2}}}g_{i_{2}}\psi_{2}+(\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}\chi_{k})\phi_{1}

\displaystyle=t^{N^{\prime}(\sum_{k=2}^{j}r_{i_{k}})+r_{i_{j}}}g_{i_{j}}\psi_{j}+\chi_{j-1}t^{N^{\prime}(\sum_{k=2}^{j-1}r_{i_{k}})+r_{i_{j-1}}}g_{i_{j-1}}\psi_{j-1}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\cdots+(\prod_{k=2}^{j-1}\chi_{k})t^{N^{\prime}r_{i_{2}}+r_{i_{2}}}g_{i_{2}}\psi_{2}+(\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}\chi_{k})t^{r_{i_{1}}}g_{i_{1}}\psi_{1}

where
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\rho}
are elements of
\mathcal{O}_{(z,0),C}
which depend on
\phi_{\rho}
. Now if
r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}
satisfy (
condition

r

.

/
r

.
+

>
ν

) for
\nu>N^{\prime}+1
, then

\displaystyle r_{i_{1}}>N^{\prime}r_{i_{2}}+r_{i_{2}}>N^{\prime}(r_{i_{2}}+r_{i_{3}})+r_{i_{3}}>\cdots>N^{\prime}(\sum_{j=2}^{\rho}r_{i_{j}})+r_{i_{\rho}}

so that

\displaystyle t^{N^{\prime}\sum_{k=2}^{j}r_{i_{k}}}\phi_{j}=t^{N^{\prime}(\sum_{k=2}^{j}r_{i_{k}})+r_{i_{j}}}\phi^{\prime}_{j}

for some
\phi^{\prime}_{j}\in\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C}
. Then
t^{N^{\prime}\sum_{k=2}^{j}r_{i_{k}}}(\phi_{j}-t^{r_{i_{j}}}\phi^{\prime}_{j})=0
. Since
t
is a non zero-divisor in
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C}
, it follows that
\phi_{j}=t^{r_{j}}\phi^{\prime}_{j}
. Consequently,
I^{(j)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\rho}}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C}\subseteq t^{r_{i_{j}}}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C}
, which completes the proof of assertion (
1
) of
C.2
.

C.2.5
.

Assertion (

) of

follows directly from the definition of

. For assertion (

. Then

. Assume now that

and

that

\displaystyle t^{r_{i^{\prime}_{k}}+N^{\prime}r_{i_{j+1}}}g_{i^{\prime}_{k}}

\displaystyle=t^{N^{\prime}r_{i_{j+1}}}f_{i^{\prime}_{k}}=\chi_{j}\phi_{j}

with
\phi_{j}\in I
, and in turn,

\displaystyle t^{N^{\prime}\sum_{j^{\prime}=2}^{j}r_{i_{j^{\prime}}}+r_{i^{\prime}_{k}}+N^{\prime}r_{i_{j+1}}}g_{i^{\prime}_{k}}

\displaystyle=t^{N^{\prime}\sum_{j^{\prime}=2}^{j}r_{i_{j^{\prime}}+r_{i_{j}}}}\phi^{\prime}_{j}

for some
\phi^{\prime}_{j}\in\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(z,0),C}
. Then under (

r

.

/
r

.
+

>
>
1

),

and consequently,
